Approximate Leave-one-out Cross Validation for Regression with ℓ 1 \ell_{1} Regularizers (extended version)
Arnab Auddy, ??, Haolin Zou, ??, Kamiar Rahnama Rad, ??, Arian Maleki ??

Abstract
The out-of-sample error (OO) is the main quantity of interest in risk estimation and model selection. Leave-one-out cross validation (LO) offers a (nearly) distribution-free yet computationally demanding approach to estimate OO. Recent theoretical work showed that approximate leave-one-out cross validation (ALO) is a computationally efficient and statistically reliable estimate of LO (and OO) for generalized linear models with differentiable regularizers. For problems involving non-differentiable regularizers, despite significant empirical evidence, the theoretical understanding of ALO’s error remains unknown. In this paper, we present a novel theory for a wide class of problems in the generalized linear model family with non-differentiable regularizers. We bound the error | ALO − LO | |{\rm ALO}-{\rm LO}| in terms of intuitive metrics such as the size of leave- i i -out perturbations in active sets, sample size n n , number of features p p and regularization parameters. As a consequence, for the ℓ 1 \ell_{1} -regularized problems, we show that | ALO − LO | → p → ∞ 0 |{\rm ALO}-{\rm LO}|\xrightarrow{p\rightarrow\infty}0 while n / p n/p and SNR are fixed and bounded.

1
Introduction

We observe a dataset
\mathcal{D}=\{(y_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1}),\dots,(y_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n})\}
where
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
denote the features and response, respectively. We assume observations are independent and identically distributed draws from some unknown joint distribution
q(y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*})p(\mathbf{x}_{i})
. We estimate
\bm{\beta}^{*}
using
the optimization problem

\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\lambda}:={\rm argmin}_{\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta})+\lambda r(\bm{\beta})

(1)

where
\ell(y|z)
is the loss function, and
r(\bm{\beta})
is the regularizer. Consider the problem of estimating the out-of-sample prediction error (OO)

{\rm OO}_{\lambda}:=\mathbb{E}[\phi(y_{\rm new},\mathbf{x}_{\rm new}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\lambda})|{\cal D}]

where
\phi(y,z)
is another loss function (possibly but not necessarily the same as
\ell(y|z)
),
(y_{\rm new},\mathbf{x}_{\rm new})
is a sample from the same joint distribution
q(y|\mathbf{x}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*})p(\mathbf{x})
, but is independent of the training set
{\cal D}
. As demonstrated through empirical and theoretical studies, in high-dimensional settings (
n,p\rightarrow\infty
while
n/p
and SNR are fixed and bounded), the leave-one-out cross validation (LO) estimator

{\rm LO}:=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(y_{i};\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i})

(2)

where

\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}={\rm argmin}_{\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\{\sum_{j\neq i}\ell(y_{j}|\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\beta})+\lambda r(\bm{\beta})\right\},

(3)

provides an accurate estimate of the risk:
Rahnama Rad et al. 2020
;
Patil et al. 2021
.

A significant limitation of LO is its necessity to fit the model repeatedly
n
times, making it computationally impractical for many high-dimensional problems. As a result, recently several researches have considered the problem of approximating LO
(
Beirami et al. 2017
;
Stephenson and Broderick 2020
;
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
;
Giordano et al. 2019b
;
Giordano et al. 2019a
;
Wang et al. 2018
;
Rahnama Rad et al. 2020
;
Patil et al. 2021
;
Patil et al. 2022
)
. For instance in
(
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
;
Rahnama Rad et al. 2020
)
it was theoretically and empirically shown that for differentiable regularizers and generalized linear models approximate leave-one-out cross validation (ALO) is a statistically reliable and computationally efficient approach to estimate LO, and OO. Regarding non-differentiable regularizers, while the extensive simulations in
(
Wang et al. 2018
;
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
)
provided empirical evidence, the theoretical understanding of ALO’s error remains unknown.

In this paper, we present a novel theory for non-differentiable regularizers applied to a wide class of problems in the generalized linear model family, e.g., Poisson and logistic. Using intuitive metrics such as the size of leave-
i
-out perturbations in active sets we bound the error
|{\rm ALO}-{\rm LO}|
in terms of fundamental quantities such as sample size
n
, number of features
p
, and the regularization parameters. For elastic net problems we place bounds on the size of leave-
i
-out perturbations in active sets, and as a consequence, show that
|{\rm ALO}-{\rm LO}|\xrightarrow{p\rightarrow\infty}0
when
n/p
and SNR remain fixed and finite
*
*

*

In Section
3.4
we rigorously define a fixed finite SNR. Roughly speaking, we mean that
\mbox{var}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}]
and
\mbox{var}[y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}]
remain fixed and finite regardless of problem dimensions.
.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we briefly present and review the key idea of ALO and the challenge to use it for non-differentiable regularizers. We review related work in section
2.3
. In section
2.4
we briefly describe the main theoretical contributions of this paper.
Section
3
formally introduces our theoretical framework and presents Theorem
3.2
which allows us to bound the error
|{\rm ALO}-{\rm LO}|
in terms of intuitive metrics such as the size of leave-
i
-out perturbations in active sets for wide array of models models such as linear Gaussian, logistic and Poisson. In Section
4
we present Theorem
4.1
which bounds metrics related to the size of leave-
i
-out perturbations in active sets for
\ell_{1}
regularized problems. Next we explain how Theorem
3.2
and Theorem
4.1
together prove that for
\ell_{1}
regularized problems, when
n
and
p
grow, as
n/p
and SNR remains fixed and finite, ALO is a consistent estimate of LO. Detailed proofs are given in Section
5
. Concluding remarks are given in section
6
. Finally, the Appendix
A
prepares some results on the elastic net regularized least squares optimization problem.

2
Approximate leave-one-out cross validation

In this section, we briefly review the main idea of ALO and we describe the challenges of using it for non-differentiable regularizers.

2.1
ALO for twice-differentiable losses and regularizers

ALO replaces the computationally demanding procedure of repeatedly fitting the model with finding an approximate model that is easy to compute. Instead of exactly computing
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}
as in (
3
), ALO adjusts the estimate
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
based on the entire dataset
{\cal D}
, and uses one Newton step to compute the approximation
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}
as follows:

\displaystyle~\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}:=\widehat{\bm{\beta}}+\left(\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbf{x}_{j}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\top}\ddot{\ell}(\mathbf{y}_{j}|\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}})+\lambda\nabla^{2}r(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\right)^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i}\dot{\ell}(y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}),

(4)

where
\dot{\ell}(y|z)
and
\ddot{\ell}(y|z)
denote the first and second derivatives of
\ell(y|z)
with respect to its second argument. Furthermore,

[\nabla^{2}r({\mathbf{w}})]_{ij}:=\left.\frac{\partial^{2}r(\bm{\beta})}{\partial\beta_{i}\partial\beta_{j}}\right|_{\bm{\beta}=\mathbf{w}}.

It might seem that the matrix inversion required in (
4
) is computationally (nearly) as demanding as refitting the model, but this can actually be bypassed by using the Woodbury lemma (see Lemma
10
), leading to the following approximation

\displaystyle{\rm ALO}:=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(y_{i};\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{i})

(5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi\left(y_{i};\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}+\left(\dfrac{\dot{\ell}(y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}})}{\ddot{\ell}(y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}})}\right)\left(\dfrac{H_{ii}}{1-H_{ii}}\right)\right)

where
H_{ii}
is the
(i,i)
element of the matrix
\mathbf{H}
defined as

\mathbf{H}:=\mathbf{X}\big(\mathbf{X}^{\top}[{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}))]\mathbf{X}+\lambda\nabla^{2}r(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\big)^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\diag[\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})]

with

\ddot{\ell}(\mathbf{w}):=\left[\ddot{\ell}(y_{1}|\mathbf{x}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{w}),\cdots,\ddot{\ell}(y_{n}|\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\top}\mathbf{w})\right]^{\top}
and
\diag[\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})]
being the diagonal matrix with
\ddot{\ell}(\mathbf{w})
as its diagonal elements.
Most of the theoretical work about the consistency of ALO in estimating LO (and OO) has focused on differentiable regularizers, such as ridge, smoothed LASSO and Huber loss when
n/p
remain fixed and finite
(
Rahnama Rad et al. 2020
;
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
;
Patil et al. 2022
;
Xu et al. 2021
)
.

2.2
ALO for non-differentiable regularizers

The ALO formula above and the corresponding theory supporting it require twice differentiability but some of the most important loss functions and regularizers, such as elastic net, nuclear norm, or hinge loss are not twice-differentiable.
For example, consider the following estimate:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\beta}}:=~

\displaystyle\underset{\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}{\rm argmin}\ h(\bm{\beta}),

(6)

where
h(\bm{\beta}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta})+\lambda(1-\eta)\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}+\lambda\eta\|\bm{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}
,
and suppose that as before our goal is to approximate

{\rm LO}:=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(y_{i};\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i})

(7)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}:=

\displaystyle\underset{\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}{\rm argmin}\left\{\sum_{j\neq i}\ell(y_{j}|\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\beta})+\lambda(1-\eta)\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}+\lambda\eta\|\bm{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}\right\}.

(8)

Due to the regularizer’s non-differentiability we cannot use the one step Newton approximation as proposed in the previous section. However, the following heuristic argument motivates our new method. Let
{\cal S}
denote the active set of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
, i.e.,

{\cal S}=\{i\ :\ \widehat{\beta}_{i}\neq 0\}.

Suppose that the active set of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}
remains the same as
{\cal S}
for all
i
. Then, we can solve (
3
) on the set
{\cal S}
only. The validity of this heuristic assumption, and our remedies when it is mildly violated, are discussed in later sections. For now, since the regularizer is twice differentiable on
{\cal S}
, we can use the Newton method to obtain the following approximation for LO of the elastic-net:

\displaystyle{\rm ALO}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi\left(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}+\left(\tfrac{\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})}{\ddot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})}\right)\left(\dfrac{H_{ii}}{1-H_{ii}}\right)\right)

(9)

where
H_{ii}
is the
(i,i)
element of

\mathbf{H}:=\mathbf{X}_{\cal S}\left(2\lambda\eta\mathbb{I}+\mathbf{X}_{\cal S}^{\top}{\rm diag}[\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})]\mathbf{X}_{\cal S}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cal S}^{\top}{\rm diag}[\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})].

with
\mathbf{X}_{{\cal S}}
contains the columns of
X
that are in
{\cal S}
.

Unfortunately, the assumption that led to (
9
), i.e., the assumption that the active set does not change when a data point is removed, is not correct. While it is true that some of leave-
i
-out estimated coefficients retain the active set, most estimated active sets do change. Figure
1
confirms this claim.

size of change in active set

-out (i.e., leaving out the
i
-th observation for
i=1,\dots,n
). The parameters are
n=500
,
p=1000
with 20% of the true coefficients being non-zero. The design matrix
\mathbf{X}
has iid
N(0,1/n)
rows, and the nonzero coefficients are iid
N(0,1)
. The penalty strengths are
\lambda=2,\eta=0.5
. We use the
ElasticNet
function from the Python library
scikit-learn
.

The figure shows boxplots of the change in sizes of leave-
i
-out active sets (denoted by
\Delta_{p}
), plotted against the dimension, (on a logarithmic scale) when performing linear regression with the elastic net penalty. The upper and lower edges of the box in each boxplot represent the 1st and the 3rd quartiles respectively, and the black line represents the median. The whiskers extend up to the most extreme value in 1.5 times the interquartile range. The parameters are taken as
p=n
(left) and
p=2n
(right), and in either figure
p
is then varied from 1000 to 10000 with six equal increments on the log scale. We take 20% of the true coefficients to be non-zero. The design matrix
\mathbf{X}
has iid
N(0,1/n)
rows, and the nonzero coefficients are iid
N(0,1)
. The penalty strengths are
\lambda=2,\eta=0.5
. We use the
ElasticNet
function from the Python library
scikit-learn
.

Despite the observation in Figure
1
, extensive empirical results presented in
Beirami et al. 2017
;
Wang et al. 2018
;
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
;
Stephenson and Broderick 2020
confirm that (
9
) offer an accurate estimation of LO (and OO).

To understand these two contradictory observations, we ran another simulation that appears in Figure
2
. In this figure we find that, while the active set does change, the number of changes in the active set (denoted by
\Delta_{p}
) scales at a sub-linear rate with respect to
p
(and hence
n
) as
p,n
increases. Indeed, the comparable points in the boxplots (e.g., the median, maximum) of the logarithms, lie on a line that has slope smaller than one. A linear regression of the medians of
\log(\Delta_{p})
on
\log(p)
showed a slope of 0.43 when
p=n
and 0.52 when
p=2n
.

This implies that
\Delta_{p}/p\to 0
as
p,n
increases. As will be clarified later, this sub-linear rate of growth that will be proved in Theorem
4.1
, is the main reason that the simulation results confirm the accuracy of (
9
).

2.3
Related work

Various approaches to estimating the out-of-sample error have been proposed. Examples include (but are not limited to) cross validation
(
Stone 1974
)
, predicted residual error sum of squares
(
Allen 1974
)
, and generalized cross validation
(
Craven and Wahba 1979
;
Golub et al. 1979
)
, just to name a few.

Until recently, the use of
n
-fold cross validation (also known as LO) has been limited due to the high computational cost of repeatedly refitting the model
n
times, and due to concerns about the high variance, especially when
n
and
p
grow unboundedly. Recently, these concerns have been (mostly) alleviated by a large body of work that showed: 1) that the variance of LO in estimating OO goes to zero as
n
and
p
grow
(
Kumar et al. 2013
;
Bayle et al. 2020
;
Rahnama Rad et al. 2020
;
Patil et al. 2022
;
Luo et al. 2023
)
, and 2) that computationally efficient approximations to the leave-one-out cross validation (can) provide statistically reliable estimates of LO and OO
(
Beirami et al. 2017
;
Wang et al. 2018
;
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
;
Rahnama Rad et al. 2020
;
Patil et al. 2022
;
Stephenson and Broderick 2020
;
Obuchi and Sakata 2019
;
Opper and Winther 2000
;
Cawley and Talbot 2008
;
Meijer and Goeman 2013
;
Vehtari et al. 2017
;
Vehtari et al. 2016
;
Obuchi and Kabashima 2016
;
Obuchi and Kabashima 2018
;
Xu et al. 2021
)
. Most theoretical work about the consistency of ALO in estimating LO has focused on differentiable regularizers, such as ridge, smoothed LASSO and Huber loss
(
Rahnama Rad et al. 2020
;
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
;
Patil et al. 2022
)
. Assuming that the SNR grows unboundedly, as
n
grows,
Stephenson and Broderick 2020
considered
\ell_{1}
regularizers. In this regime the optimal value of the regularization parameter
\lambda
goes to zero, and tuning becomes (nearly) unimportant because of the unbounded SNR.

Despite significant empirical evidence, to the best of our knowledge, there is no theoretical study of the consistency of ALO for non-differentiable regularizers in a regime where
n
and
p
grow to infinity while
n/p
and SNR remains bounded, a framework typical in high dimensional risk estimation problems
(
Donoho et al. 2011a
;
Donoho and Montanari 2016
;
Maleki 2011
;
Mousavi et al. 2018
;
Wang et al. 2020
;
Wang et al. 2022
;
Xu et al. 2021
;
Guo et al. 2022
;
Rahnama Rad et al. 2020
;
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
;
Patil et al. 2022
)
. Given the importance of risk estimation for non-differertiable estimation problems, this paper addresses the problem in the finite-SNR regime where tuning significantly impacts the selected model and estimated coefficients (as we discuss in Section
3.2
).

2.4
Our technical contributions

In this paper, our primary focus is on risk estimation for
\ell_{1}
regularized problems within the generalized linear model family. Specifically, we aim to establish an upper bound for the error
|{\rm ALO}-{\rm LO}|
under the conditions of large
n
and
p
, while maintaining fixed and bounded values for
n/p
and SNR. In the following, we outline some of the key theoretical innovations that have enabled us to undertake a comprehensive analysis of
|{\rm ALO}-{\rm LO}|
in this context.

Our initial step involves a smooth approximation
r_{\alpha}(\mathbf{z})
for the
\ell_{1}
-norm
\|\mathbf{z}\|_{1}
, where
\alpha
is a parameter such that, as
\alpha
approaches infinity, this approximation becomes increasingly accurate. While smoothing techniques have been extensively employed for deriving approximate minimizers of non-differentiable convex functions, their application as proof techniques in high-dimensional statistics has been unexplored. The primary challenge arises from the fact that as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
, it becomes considerably difficult to bound the quantities that are of particular interest to statisticians, such as
|{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}-{\rm LO}^{\alpha}|
in our specific problem. Here,
{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}
and
{\rm LO}^{\alpha}
represent the smoothed approximations of
{\rm ALO}
and
{\rm LO}
, respectively.

In our paper (in the proof of Theorem
3.2
), we have devised an innovative method to bound such quantities, which we anticipate to have broader applications in studying non-differentiable losses and regularizers in high-dimensional settings.

Our smoothing technique, in essence, simplifies the task of bounding
|{\rm ALO}-{\rm LO}|
by transforming it into the challenge of limiting the changes in the active set between the full-data estimate and the leave-one-out estimate. Hence, in this paper, we develop new techniques for understanding the relationships between two estimates that are using the same samples (proof of Theorem
4.1
). As an example of our approach, we employ our technique to establish an upper bound for the disparity between
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}_{/\penalty i}
, where
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}_{/\penalty i}
denote the locations of non-zero coefficients in
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/\penalty i}
, respectively. We anticipate that this technique will prove valuable for other problems, such as the analysis of ensemble methods within the context of high-dimensional settings.

3
Main theoretical result

We begin by introducing our notation in Section
3.1
and assumptions in Section
3.2
. We discuss the assumptions in Section
3.3
and show these assumption encompass a large class of typical problems. We discuss the issue of bounded signal-to-noise ratio in Section
3.4
. Then we present our main theorem in Section
3.5
.

3.1
Notations

In this manuscript, vectors are denoted with boldfaced lowercase letter, such as
\mathbf{x}
. Matrices are represented with Boldfaced capital letters, such as
\mathbf{X}
. Calligraphic letters, such as
{\cal F}
are used for sets and events. One exception is that we use
[n]
for
\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
. For matrix
\mathbf{X}
,
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{X})
,
\|\mathbf{X}\|
,
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{HS}
,
{\rm Tr}(\mathbf{X})
denote the minimum signular value, the spectral norm (maximum singular value), the Hilbert-Schmidt norm, and the trace of the matrix
\mathbf{X}
respectively. Suppose
{\cal F}
represents a subset of indices corresponding to the columns of matrix
\mathbf{X}
. In such a case, the notation
\mathbf{X}_{{\cal F}}
refers to a matrix formed by selecting only those columns of
\mathbf{X}
whose indices belong to the set
{\cal F}
. The subscript “
/i
” refers a quantity related to the leave-
i
-out model, e.g.
\mathbf{X}_{/i}
refers to matrix
\mathbf{X}
after deleting the
i
th row, and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}
,
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}
refer to the leave-
i
-out estimate and the smoothed leave-
i
-out estimate, respectively. For two probability measures
\mu,\nu
,
W_{q}(\mu,\nu)
denote their Wasserstein-
q
distance. Moreover, we use the following definitions in this paper:

\displaystyle\dot{\ell}_{i}(\bm{\beta})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ell(y_{i}|z)}{\partial z}\bigg|_{z=\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}},\quad\ddot{\ell}_{i}(\bm{\beta}):=\frac{\partial^{2}\ell(y_{i}|z)}{\partial z^{2}}\bigg|_{z=\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}}

\displaystyle\dot{\ell}_{/i}(\bm{\beta})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left[\dot{\ell}_{1}(\bm{\beta}),\cdots,\dot{\ell}_{i-1}(\bm{\beta}),\dot{\ell}_{i+1}(\bm{\beta}),\cdots,,\dot{\ell}_{n}(\bm{\beta})\right]^{\top},

\displaystyle\ddot{\ell}_{/i}(\bm{\beta})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left[\ddot{\ell}_{1}(\bm{\beta}),\cdots,\ddot{\ell}_{i-1}(\bm{\beta}),\ddot{\ell}_{i+1}(\bm{\beta}),\cdots,,\ddot{\ell}_{n}(\bm{\beta})\right]^{\top}.

We also denote any polynomials of
\log(n)
by
{\rm PolyLog}(n)
. For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
x\wedge y
and
x\vee y
to denote
\min\{x,y\}
and
\max\{x,y\}
respectively. We use the following classic notations for convergence rates: for a sequence of real numbers
a_{n}
,
a_{n}=o(1)
iff
a_{n}\to 0
,
a_{n}=O(1)
iff
|a_{n}|\leq C
with
C<\infty
for all sufficiently large
n
,
a_{n}=\omega(1)
iff
a_{n}\to\infty
, and
a_{n}=\Theta(1)
iff
0<C_{1}<|a_{n}|<C_{2}
for all sufficiently large
n
, with
C_{2}<\infty
. For two sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
,
a_{n}=o(b_{n})
iff
\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}=o(1)
, and the other notations are similar. We use similar notations for convergence in probability, e.g.
X_{n}=o_{p}(1)
iff
X_{n}\to 0
in probability, and so forth.

3.2
Assumption group A

The following assumptions have been extensively used in the literature of high-dimensional statistics. We will explain the rationale for making these assumptions in the next section.

A1

\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{x}_{n})^{\top}
where
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
are iid
N(0,\bm{\Sigma})
. Moreover, there exists constants
0<c_{X}\leq C_{X}
such that
p^{-1}c_{X}\leq\sigma_{\min}(\bm{\Sigma})\leq\sigma_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma})\leq p^{-1}C_{X}
.

A2

n/p=\gamma_{0}\in(0,\infty)
.

A3

\phi
has continuous derivative
\dot{\phi}
, and
\ell(y|z)
has continuous second derivative w.r.t.
z
.

A4

There exists
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{\epsilon}}>0
, and
q_{n},\check{q}_{n},\bar{q}_{n}\in[0,1)
, such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\underset{\mathbf{v}\in{\cal D}}{1\leq i\leq n}}\dot{\ell}_{i}(\mathbf{v})\leq{\rm PolyLog}(n)\right)\geq 1-\check{q}_{n},

(10)

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\underset{\mathbf{v}\in{\cal D}}{1\leq i\leq n}}\ddot{\ell}_{i}(\mathbf{v})\leq{\rm PolyLog}(n)\right)\geq 1-q_{n},

(11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n,\\
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in{\cal D}\end{subarray}}\frac{\|\ddot{\ell}_{i}(\mathbf{v})-\ddot{\ell}_{/i}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\|}{\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|_{2}}\leq{\rm PolyLog}(n)\right)

\displaystyle~\geq 1-\bar{q}_{n}

(12)

where

\displaystyle{\cal D}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\underset{1\leq i\leq n}{\cup}\underset{t\in[0,1]}{\cup}{\cal B}(t\widehat{\bm{\beta}}+(1-t)\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{\epsilon}}),

\displaystyle{\cal B}(\mathbf{w},r)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{z}:\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{w}\|_{2}\leq r\right\}.

A5

\eta\in(0,1)
, and
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{\max}]
for an arbitrary constant
\lambda_{\max}>0
.

3.3
Discussion of the assumptions

In the last section, we made five assumptions that will be used for our theoretical results. In this section, we would like to clarify our rationale for making these assumptions.

3.3.1
About assumption A1 and A2

To ensures that the
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}
typically remains finite as
n
and
p
grow unboundedly as long as
\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2}/p
is fixed:

\frac{c_{X}}{p}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*})^{2}\leq\frac{C_{X}}{p}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2}.

For instance, if each element of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
remains bounded, then
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}
will be
O_{p}(1)
.

In addition to the aforementioned rationale for selecting the presented, there is another compelling justification that we explain below.

Let
\lambda^{*}(n,p)
denote the value of
\lambda
that minimizes the prediction error of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\lambda}
. Suppose that we are interested in the asymptotic setting
n,p\rightarrow\infty
, such that
n/p=\gamma_{0}
remains fixed. Then, if Assumption A1 holds, for many problems it has been shown that
\lambda^{*}(n,p)\rightarrow\bar{\lambda}
, in probability, where
\bar{\lambda}
is a fixed number in the range
(0,\infty)
. See for instance
Mousavi et al. 2018
;
Wang et al. 2020
;
Wang et al. 2022
. Inutitively speaking, if under another scaling
\lambda^{*}(n,p)
goes to zero as
n,p\rightarrow\infty
, that is an indication of the fact that the estimation problem is becoming easier as
p
grows and hence a regularizer is not required. Similarly, if under another scaling
\lambda^{*}(n,p)
goes to infinity as
n,p\rightarrow\infty
, that is an indication of the fact that the estimation problem is becoming so difficult that we end up choosing zero estimator as the best one. Hence, the scaling we have chosen here seems to be one of the most useful scalings for practice.

In summary, we believe that Assumptions A1 and A2 provide a good scaling regime for studying risk estimation (or the related problem of hyperparameter tuning) problem.

3.3.2
About assumption A4

To introduce a regularity condition for the data generating mechanism and the loss function
\ell
. To clarify this point, we present a proposition below, which outlines a sufficient condition based on simpler regularity conditions for
\ell
and the data generation mechanism to satisfy A4.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{P}(|y_{i}|>{\rm PolyLog}(n))\leq q_{n}^{y}
for some
q_{n}^{y}=o(1/n)
. Furthermore, suppose that
\ell(y|z)
is three times differentiable with respect to
z
and that
\ell(y|z)
,
\dot{\ell}(y|z)
,
\ddot{\ell}(y|z),
and
\dddot{\ell}(y|z)
grow polynomially in
y,z
, i.e., there exists a positive integer
m
and a constant
C>0
such that

\max\{|\ell(y|z)|,|\dot{\ell}(y|z)|,|\ddot{\ell}(y|z)|,|\dddot{\ell}(y|z)|\}\leq C(1+|y|^{m}+|z|^{m})

for all
(y,z)
. Then, Assumption A4 holds.

The proof of this result is presented in Section
5.8
. The condition of polynomial growth for the loss function is not unduly restrictive, as it encompasses many commonly used loss functions. To illustrate this point, we present a few examples of popular loss functions, viz., squared error, logistic, and Poisson. Therefore, Assumption A4 holds for the majority of loss functions encountered in applications.

For simplicity we assume
\mathbf{x}_{i}\sim N(0,\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{I}_{p})
, but the examples are still valid for a general covarance matrix as in Assumption A1.

Example 3.1

(Linear Model)
.

Suppose
y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}\sim N(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*},\sigma^{2})
, then
y_{i}\sim N(0,\sigma^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2})
. Denote
\nu^{2}:=\sigma^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2}
, we then have, for arbitrary
q>1
:

\mathbb{P}(|y_{i}|>\nu\sqrt{2q\log(n)})\leq 2e^{-\frac{2q\nu^{2}\log(n)}{2\nu^{2}}}=n^{-q}.

If we use negative log-likelihood as loss function, then
\ell(y|z)
and its derivatives w.r.t.
z
are:

\displaystyle\ell(y|z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(y-z)^{2},

\displaystyle\dot{\ell}(y|z)=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}(z-y)\,;\,\ddot{\ell}(y|z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\,;\,\dddot{\ell}(y|z)=0,

and hence they are all dominated by
\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}(1+y^{2}+z^{2})
.

Example 3.2

(Logistic regression)
.

Suppose
y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}\sim Bernoulli(1/1+e^{-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}})
, then the boundedness of
|y_{i}|
is natually satisfied since
y_{i}\in\{0,1\}
. The negative log-likelihood loss and its derivatives w.r.t.
z
are

\displaystyle|\ell(y|z)|

\displaystyle=\left\lvert y\log(1+e^{-z})+(1-y)\log(1+e^{z})|\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\log(2)+2|z|,

\displaystyle|\dot{\ell}(y|z)|

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{e^{z}}{1+e^{z}}-y\right\rvert\leq 1+|y|,

\displaystyle|\ddot{\ell}(y|z)|

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{e^{-z}}{(1+e^{-z})^{2}}\right\rvert\leq 1,

\displaystyle|\dddot{\ell}(y|z)|

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{e^{z}-e^{-z}}{(e^{-z}+e^{z}+2)^{2}}\right\rvert\leq 4.

The bound of
\ell(y|z)
uses the fact that
y\in\{0,1\}
.

Example 3.3

(Poisson Regression)
.

Suppose
y_{i}\sim Poisson(\lambda)
where
\lambda=\log(1+e^{\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}})
, then we have, by Chernoff bound (see, e.g., Exercise 2.3.3 of
Vershynin 2018
):

\mathbb{P}(y_{i}>t)\leq e^{-\lambda}\left(\frac{e\lambda}{t}\right)^{t}.

Since
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}\sim N(0,\frac{1}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2})
, we have

\mathbb{P}(|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}|>2\nu\sqrt{\log(n)})\leq n^{-2}

where
\nu^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2}
. So we have, with probability at least
1-n^{-2}
, that

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\log(1+e^{\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}})\leq\log(2)+|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}|

\displaystyle\leq\log(2)+2n^{-1/2}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|\sqrt{\log(n)}.

Setting
t=\log(n)
in (
3.3
), since
\lambda>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(y_{i}>\log(n))

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{e(\log(2)+2\nu\sqrt{\log(n)})}{\log(n)}\right)^{\log(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{C}{\sqrt{\log(n)}}\right)^{\log(n)}=o(n^{-1}).

It can be checked that the negative log-likelihood loss is

\displaystyle|\ell(y|z)|

\displaystyle=|\log(y!)+\log(1+e^{z})-y\log\log(1+e^{z})|

\displaystyle\leq C(y^{2}+z^{2}+1)

where
C=1+\log\log(2)+(2\log(2))^{-1}
.

The derivatives of the loss function w.r.t.
z
satisfy

\displaystyle|\dot{\ell}(y|z)|=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{1}{1+e^{-z}}-\frac{ye^{z}}{(1+e^{z})\log(1+e^{z})}\right\rvert\leq 1+|y|,

\displaystyle|\ddot{\ell}(y|z)|=

\displaystyle~\bigg|y\left(\dfrac{e^{z}}{(1+e^{z})\log(1+e^{z})}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle~-\dfrac{ye^{z}}{(1+e^{z})^{2}\log(1+e^{z})}+\dfrac{e^{z}}{(1+e^{z})^{2}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~1+2|y|,

\displaystyle|\dddot{\ell}(y|z)|\leq

\displaystyle~3+14|y|.

We use the fact that
((1+e^{-z})\log(1+e^{z}))^{-1}\leq 1
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
. We omit the exact expression of
\dddot{\ell}(y|z)
for brevity.

3.4
Bounded Signal to Noise Ratio

We briefly explain the point of ‘bounded signal to noise ratio (SNR)’ mentioned in the introduction. Although our method and the conclusion of this paper do not rely explicitly on the
{\rm SNR}
, the notion of keeping a delicate balance between the signal and noise is one of the most important foundations of our theory. We define the signal to noise ratio as

{\rm SNR}=\frac{\mbox{var}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*})}{\mbox{var}(y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*})}.

It can be shown that the
{\rm SNR}
is
O_{p}(1)
in the three GLM examples in the previous section. In fact, under Assumption A1

\mbox{var}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*})=(\bm{\beta}^{*})^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\beta}^{*}\leq\frac{C_{X}}{p}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}=O(1)

and for the three generalized linear models (linear, logistic, and Poisson regression) in the previous subsection, we have

\dfrac{1}{\mbox{var}(y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}))}=\begin{cases}\sigma^{-2}\,&\text{for linear}\\
\left({\rm e}^{-\frac{\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}}{2}}+{\rm e}^{\frac{\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}}{2}}\right)^{2}\,&\text{for logistic}\\
(\log(1+\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}))^{-1}\,&\text{for Poisson}\end{cases}

all of which are
O_{p}(1)
using the fact that

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}

\displaystyle\sim N(0,C_{X}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}/p)=O_{p}(1),

\displaystyle 2\leq{\rm e}^{-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}}

\displaystyle+{\rm e}^{\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}}+2\leq 2({\rm e}^{|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{*}|}+1)=O_{p}(1).

Moreover if we fix
\frac{1}{p}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}=\xi
, then
{\rm SNR}^{-1}
is also
O_{p}(1)
.

3.5
Main theorem

In addition to Assumptions A1-A5, our main theorem uses two important sets related to the active set of
\widehat{}\bm{\beta}
and
\widehat{}\bm{\beta}_{/i}
. In the definition of one of these sets we require the notion of the subgradient vector
g(\bm{\beta})
that we introduce below. Define
g(\bm{\beta})
as

\displaystyle g(\bm{\beta}):=\frac{1}{\lambda(1-\eta)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\ell}(\mathbf{y}_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta})-\frac{2\eta}{1-\eta}\bm{\beta}

(14)

It can be directly verified that

g(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\in\partial\lVert\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\rVert_{1}
belongs to the subgradient of
\ell_{1}
-term in (
6
).
In fact, the first order derivative of (
6
) gives:

0\in\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\ell}(\mathbf{y}_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}})+\lambda(1-\eta)\partial\lVert\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\rVert_{1}+2\lambda\eta\widehat{\bm{\beta}}

where
\partial\lVert\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\rVert_{1}
denotes the subgradient of
\lVert\bm{\beta}\rVert_{1}
evaluated at
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
. We then obtain (
14
) by rearranging the terms. Hence we hereafter refer to
g(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})
as ‘the’ subgradient of
\lVert\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\rVert_{1}
. Based on the subgradient, define the following subsets of
[p]=\{1,2,3,\ldots,p\}
:

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(1)}

\displaystyle~:=\{k\in[p]:|\widehat{\beta}_{k}|>\kappa_{1}(n)\},

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(0)}

\displaystyle~:=\{k\in[p]:|g(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})_{k}|\leq 1-\kappa_{0}(n)\},

(15)

where
\kappa_{1}(n)
and
\kappa_{0}(n)
both are
o(1)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
†
†

†

eg.
\kappa_{0}(n)=(\log p)^{1/6}p^{-\delta}
where
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{6})
, and
\kappa_{1}(n)=p^{-1/12}
as we will see in later sections.
We will clarify our choice of these parameters later.
Likewise, we can define
{\cal S}_{/i}^{(1)},{\cal S}_{/i}^{(0)}
for the leave-
i
-out problems.

Note that
{\cal S}^{(1)}
is a subset of the active set of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
by only including active elements that are not too close to zero. On the one hand, the condition
\kappa_{1}(n)\to 0
implies that
{\cal S}^{(1)}
is close to the active set. On the other hand, when the gap
\kappa_{1}(n)
is selected to be sufficiently large (the choice will be clarified later), it is intuitively expected that only a very small fraction of the indices in
{\cal S}^{(1)}
will move out of the active set (i.e. the corresponding coefficient becomes zero) in the leave-
i
-out problem. These two points makes
{\cal S}^{(1)}
a good substitution of the active set discussed in Section
2.2
.

To understand
{\cal S}^{(0)}
, consider the non-active set of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
, i.e. the indices of zero coefficients of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
.
{\cal S}^{(0)}
is actually a subset of the non-active set, which only includes elements with sub-gradients bounded away from 1, as indicated by its definition
\{k\in[p]:|g(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})_{k}|\leq 1-\kappa_{0}(n)\}
. Again
\kappa_{0}(n)\to 0
makes
{\cal S}^{(0)}
close to the non-active set, and by setting the right rate of the convergence of
\kappa_{0}(n)
, it should be expected that only a very small number of regression coefficients corresponding to indices in
{\cal S}^{(0)}
will become nonzero in the leave-
i
-out problem.

To sum up, our choice of
{\cal S}^{(1)}
and
{\cal S}^{(0)}
serve as proxies of the active and non-active set of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
that are more resilient to the leave-
i
-out procedure. In other words, we expect the size of
({\cal S}^{(1)}\cup{\cal S}^{(0)})^{c}
to be small compared to
p
. As is clear from the above discussion, the speed at which
\kappa_{1}(n)
and
\kappa_{0}(n)
go to zero must be carefully selected to satisfy the two contrasting objectives. On the one hand, we want them both to go to zero as slowly as possible so that
{\cal S}^{(1)}
and
{\cal S}^{(0)}
only include elements from which we have strong evidence to be active and non-active, respectively. On the other hand, we want
\kappa_{1}(n)
and
\kappa_{0}(n)
to go to zero as fast as possible, so that
({\cal S}^{(1)}\cup{\cal S}^{(0)})^{c}
is as small as possible. The details are presented in Theorem
4.1
.

To describe our first theoretical result, consider the following sets:

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{1,i}:={\cal S}^{(1)}\cap{\cal S}^{(1)}_{/i},{\cal B}_{0,i}:={\cal S}^{(0)}\cap{\cal S}^{(0)}_{/i}

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{1,i,+}:={\cal B}_{1,i}\cap\{k:\widehat{\beta}_{k}\cdot\widehat{\beta}_{/i,k}>0\}.

(16)

Heuristically,
{\cal B}_{1,i}
contains the indices of large coefficients that remain large after leaving the
i^{\rm th}
observation out, and
{\cal B}_{0,i}
contains the indices of zero coefficients with small subderivative (of
\ell_{1}
-norm) that remain so after leaving the
i^{\rm th}
observation out. Note that
{\cal B}_{1,i}
and
{\cal B}_{0,i}
are mutually exclusive, and
{\cal B}_{1,i,+}
is the subset of
{\cal B}_{1,i}
that rules out the coefficients with flipped signs. Ideally we wish leaving-i-out would not change the fact of each coefficient being zero or non-zero, i.e.,
{\cal B}_{1,i}\cup{\cal B}_{0,i}=[p]
. This is clearly not true according to Figure
1
, but the violation is not substantial. Indeed, Figure
2
shows that the size of the leave-
i
-out perturbations, i.e.,
|({\cal B}_{1,i,+}\cup{\cal B}_{0,i})^{c}|
scale at a rate which is slower than
p
. We rigorously prove that
({\cal B}_{1,i,+}\cup{\cal B}_{0,i})^{c}
is indeed a small set under reasonable assumptions (see Theorem
4.1
). Our main theoretical result proves that the difference between LO and the ALO formula of (
9
) is proportional to the size of
({\cal B}_{1,i}\cup{\cal B}_{0,i})^{c}
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Under Assumptions A1-A5, for a sufficiently large constant
C>0
, let
1\leq d_{n}\leq p/C
be such that

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|({\cal B}_{0,i}\cup{\cal B}_{1,i,+})^{c}|\leq d_{n}

with probability at least
\tilde{q}_{n}
. Then we have

\displaystyle~|{\rm ALO}-{\rm LO}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{3}\eta^{3}(1\wedge\lambda\eta)^{3}}\sqrt{\dfrac{d_{n}}{n\lambda\eta}}+\dfrac{d_{n}{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{n\lambda^{2}\eta^{2}}+\sqrt{\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{n\lambda\eta}}

with probability at least
1-(n+1){\rm e}^{-p}-(n+2)p^{-d_{n}}-2q_{n}-2\check{q}_{n}-2\bar{q}_{n}-2\tilde{q}_{n}
.

Before we prove the result let us clarify the statement of the theorem. First note that while the bound we have obtained for the difference between ALO and LO is a finite sample bound, one way to interpret and understand the result is through the asymptotic setting we described in Section
3.3.1
, i.e.
n,p\rightarrow\infty
while
n/p=\gamma_{0}
. Under this asymptotic regime, as argued in
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
for twice differentiable losses and regularizers, LO offers a consistent estimate of out-of-sample risk estimate, while any
K
-fold cross validation for
K
fixed does not. According to Theorem
3.2
, if
d_{n}=o(p^{\zeta})
with
\zeta<1
, the the upper bound of Theorem
3.2
goes to zero as
n,p\rightarrow\infty
. The growth rate of
d_{n}
will be discussed in Section
4
.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

As we mentioned in the introduction, one of ingredients of the proof is the smoothing idea that we would like to describe first. Define

h(\bm{\beta}):=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(y_{i}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta})+\lambda(1-\eta)\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}+\lambda\eta\|\bm{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}\right\}.

(17)

If
h(\bm{\beta})
were a differentiable function we could use one step of the Newton method to obtain an approximate leave-one-out estimate. However, the main issue here is the non-differentiability of
\|\cdot\|_{1}
. For that reason we start with approximating the
\|\cdot\|_{1}
with a smooth function.
Let

r_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z)=\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(\log(1+e^{\alpha z})+\log(1+e^{-\alpha z})\right)

(18)

denote the
\alpha
-smoothed
\ell_{1}
regularizer. The following lemma proved in
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
shows the accuracy of this approximation:

Lemma 1

(Lemma 13 in
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
)
.

If
r_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z)
denotes the
\alpha
-smoothed
\ell_{1}
regularizer. Then we have

r_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z)\geq\lvert z\rvert,

and

\sup_{z}\lvert r_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z)-\lvert z\rvert\rvert\leq\frac{2\log 2}{\alpha}.

This lemma suggests that for large values of
\alpha
,
r_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z)
can be a good approximation of
|z|
. Hence, based on this approximation we now introduce the smoothed cost function

h_{\alpha}(\bm{\beta})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\ell(y_{i};\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta})+\lambda r_{\alpha}(\bm{\beta}),

(19)

where

r_{\alpha}(\bm{\beta}):=(1-\eta)\sum_{i=1}^{p}r^{(1)}_{\alpha}(\beta_{i})+\eta\bm{\beta}^{\top}\bm{\beta}

(20)

denotes the smoothed regularizer. If we define

\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}=\arg\min_{\bm{\beta}}h_{\alpha}(\bm{\beta}),

(21)

and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i}
as its leave-one-out estimate, then we can use Theorem 3 of
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
to prove that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}-\left(\frac{\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})}{\ddot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})}\right)\left(\frac{H^{\alpha}_{ii}}{1-H^{\alpha}_{ii}}\right)\right\rvert

(22)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{0}(\alpha){\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\sqrt{p}},\hskip 113.81102pt

where
\mathbf{H}^{\alpha}
is defined as

\small{\mathbf{X}\left(\lambda{\rm diag}[\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})]+\mathbf{X}^{\top}[{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\bm{\beta}^{\alpha}))]\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}{\rm diag}[\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})].}

(23)

For completeness we have mentioned Theorem 3 of
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
in Section
5
(Theorem
5.2
).
The main issue in the approximation of (
22
) is that
C_{0}(\alpha)\rightarrow\infty
as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
. This creates a dilemma. On one hand we would like
\alpha
to be large to make
|r^{(1)}_{\alpha}(z)-|z||
small. But on the other hand, the upper bound in Theorem 3 of
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
goes to infinity as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
. In addition to these two problems, as will be discussed later, some of the elements of
{\rm diag}[\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})]
go to infinity as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
that may cause inaccuracies and instabilities if this procedure is used in practice. Despite the fact that smoothing idea is not useful for approximating the leave-one-out risk of the elastic net, as will be shown in this proof, it still serves as a good theoretical tool for proving the accuracy of (
9
). Hence, we pursue two goals here:

•

Use a different strategy than the one pursued in
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
to find an upper bound on
\underset{1\leq i\leq n}{\max}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}-\left(\frac{\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})}{\ddot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})}\right)\left(\frac{H^{\alpha}_{ii}}{1-H^{\alpha}_{ii}}\right)\right\rvert
. Our new bounds will not go off to infinity as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
.

•

We will then prove that for large values of
\alpha
,
\mathbf{H}^{\alpha}_{ii}
is close to
\mathbf{H}_{ii}
used in (
9
).

To understand the challenge for achieving the above two goals, let us start with the following lemma:

Lemma 2

(Lemma 14 in
Rahnama Rad and Maleki 2020
)
.

r_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z)
is infinitely many times differentiable, and

\dot{r}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z)=\frac{e^{\alpha z}-e^{-\alpha z}}{e^{\alpha z}+e^{-\alpha z}+2},

\ddot{r}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z)=\frac{2\alpha}{(e^{\alpha z}+e^{-\alpha z}+2)^{2}}.

Suppose that
z_{\alpha}=O(\frac{1}{\alpha})
. Then, as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
,
\ddot{r}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(z)\rightarrow\infty
. It may seem to the reader that
z_{\alpha}=O(\frac{1}{\alpha})
is a condition that may not happen and hence it won’t cause any issues. However, this is not the case. In fact, as will be shown in the next lemma, many of the regression coefficients satify the condition
|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i,k}^{\alpha}|=O(\frac{1}{\alpha}\log p)
. For these elements as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
,
\ddot{r}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i,k}^{\alpha})\rightarrow\infty
.

Lemma 3
.

Suppose Assumptions A1-A5 hold. Let
{\cal S}^{(1)}
and
{\cal S}^{(0)}
be as defined in (
3.5
).
Then we have:

1.

\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}\|\leq\sqrt{\dfrac{4\log(2)p}{\alpha\eta}}
.

2.

\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i}\|\leq\frac{|\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|}{2\lambda\eta}

3.

\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}\|\leq\frac{|\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})|\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|}{2\lambda\eta}
.

4.

\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}\lVert g(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})-g(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i})\rVert\leq\frac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{2}\eta(1-\eta)}
with probability at least
1-q_{n}-{\rm e}^{-p}-\check{q}_{n}-ne^{-p/2}
.

5.

Suppose
\alpha=\omega\left(\frac{p}{\kappa_{1}^{2}\eta}\right)
, then for large enough
p
,

\min\limits_{0\leq i\leq n}\min\limits_{k\in{\cal S}^{(1)}_{/i}}|\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{/i,k}|\geq\dfrac{\kappa_{1}}{2}.

6.

Suppose
\alpha=\omega\left(\frac{n{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\kappa_{0}^{2}\lambda^{2}(1-\eta)\eta}\right)
, then for large enough
p
, with probability at least
1-q_{n}-e^{-p}
:

\max_{0\leq i\leq n}\max\limits_{k\in{\cal S}^{(0)}_{/i}}|\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{/i,k}|\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{4}{\kappa_{0}}\right).

The proof of this lemma is presented in Section
5.2
.

Part (1) of this lemma confirms what we expected. In fact the difference
\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}\|
shrinks as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
. We will use this bound later in our proof.

Part (6) of Lemma
3
confirms the claim we made before the proof. If
k\in{\cal S}_{/i}^{(0)}
then
|\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{/i,k}|
is proportional to
\alpha^{-1}
. Hence, combined with
2
we expect the second derivative of the smoothed regularizer at
|\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{/i,k}|
to go to infinity as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
fast enough. Note that this issue does not happen for
k\in{\cal S}_{/i}^{(1)}
as confirmed by part (5) of this lemma; In fact, for
k\in{\cal S}_{/i}^{(1)}
since
|\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{/i,k}|
is bounded away from zero, we can use Lemma
2
to show that as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
,
\ddot{r}(\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{/i,k})
goes to zero.

Another source of difficulty in handling the smoothed regularizer is that we do not have much control over the curvature,
\ddot{r}_{\alpha}^{(1)}(\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{/i,k})
when
k\in({\cal S}_{/i}^{(1)}\cup{\cal S}_{/i}^{(0)})^{c}
. Keeping this issue in mind, let us first simplify the error between the leave-one-out cross validation risk and the ALO for the smoothed problem. To simplify the calculations we introduce the following notations. Let
\dot{r}_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})
denote the vector
[\dot{r}_{\alpha}(\theta_{1}),\dot{r}_{\alpha}(\theta_{2}),\ldots,\dot{r}_{\alpha}(\theta_{p})]^{\top}
. Similarly,

\dot{\ell}({\bm{\theta}}):=[\dot{\ell}(y_{1};{\mathbf{x}}_{1}^{\top}\bm{\theta}),\dot{\ell}(y_{2};\mathbf{x}_{2}^{\top}\bm{\theta}),\ldots,\dot{\ell}(y_{n};\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\top}\bm{\theta})]^{T}

and
\dot{\ell}_{/i}({\bm{\theta}})
is the same vector as
\dot{\ell}({\bm{\theta}})
except that its
i^{\rm th}
element is removed. Furthermore, define
\mathbf{f}_{/i}(\bm{\theta})
as the gradient of
h_{\alpha}(\cdot)
at
\bm{\theta}
, i.e.,

\mathbf{f}_{/i}(\bm{\theta}):=\lambda\dot{r}_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})+\mathbf{X}_{/i}^{\top}\dot{\ell}_{/i}(\bm{\theta}).

(24)

Notice that
\mathbf{f}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i})=0
, where
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i}
is the true LO estimate.
Similarly, define the Hessian of
h_{\alpha}(\cdot)
and its leave-one-out coounter part as

\mathbf{J}(\bm{\theta})=\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta}))+\mathbf{X}^{\top}{\rm diag}[\ddot{\ell}(\bm{\theta})]\mathbf{X},

(25)

and

\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\bm{\theta})=\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta}))+\mathbf{X}_{/i}^{\top}{\rm diag}[\ddot{\ell}_{/i}(\bm{\theta})]\mathbf{X}_{/i}.

(26)

By using the first order optimality conditions, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(27)

Define
\Delta^{\alpha}_{/i}:=\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}
. Using the multivariate mean-value theorem we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})

(28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}+{\Delta^{*}}_{/i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})+\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}+t\Delta^{\alpha}_{/i})dt\right)\Delta^{\alpha}_{/i}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda{\dot{r}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})+{X}^{\top}\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})=\mathbf{f}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})+\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}){\mathbf{x}_{i}}.

(29)

Combining (
28
) and (
29
) we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha}=\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}

(30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{J}_{/i}(t\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}+(1-t)\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})dt\right)^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i}.\hskip 14.22636pt

As is clear from (
4
), the ALO approximation of
\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha}
is given by

\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}^{\alpha}=\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})\left(\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}-\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha})\right)^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i}.

(31)

Also, it is straightforward to see that

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}-{\rm LO}^{\alpha}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\phi(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i})-\phi(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}+\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}^{\alpha}))

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\lvert\dot{\phi}(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{i}^{\alpha})\right\rvert\cdot\dfrac{1}{n}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\Delta_{i}^{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}^{\alpha}\right\rvert,

(32)

where
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{i}
is a point on the line that connects
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i}
. Similar to the proof of Proposition
3.1
, we can see that for many reasonable models,
\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\lvert\dot{\phi}(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{i}^{\alpha})\right\rvert=O_{p}({\rm PolyLog}(n))
. Hence, it is enough to obtain an upper bound for
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\Delta_{i}^{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}^{\alpha}
.
From equations (
30
) and (
31
), we have

\displaystyle~\left\lvert\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}^{\alpha}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\,|\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\times

\displaystyle~\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bigg[\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{J}_{/i}(t\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}+(1-t)\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})dt\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\left(\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}-\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha})\right)^{-1}\bigg]\mathbf{x}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\,|\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\times

\displaystyle~\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bigg[\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{J}_{/i}(t\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}+(1-t)\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})dt\right)^{-1}-\left(\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})\right)^{-1}\bigg]\mathbf{x}_{i}

\displaystyle\,+|\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\left[\left(\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})\right)^{-1}-\left(\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}-\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha})\right)^{-1}\right]\mathbf{x}_{i}.

(33)

We again emphasize that we cannot let
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
in these expressions, since some of the elements of
\mathbf{J}
matrix go to infinity. Hence we have to find proper ways for obtaining an upper bound for (
3.5
) for large values of
\alpha
. As is clear from this discussion, we have to be careful about
\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{/i,k})
. The next lemma provides some information about these quantities:

Lemma 4
.

Suppose the assumptions of Lemma
3
hold, and assume

\alpha=\omega\left(\frac{n{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\kappa_{0}^{2}\lambda^{2}(1-\eta)\eta}\vee\frac{p}{\kappa_{1}^{2}\eta}\right)
.
Then the following statements hold with probability at least
1-q_{n}-e^{-p}
:

1.

\forall i,\forall k\in{\cal B}_{0,i}
:

\int_{0}^{1}\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{/i,k}-t\Delta^{\alpha}_{/i,k})dt\geq 2\eta+\frac{1}{8}\alpha(1-\eta)\kappa_{0}

2.

\forall i,\forall k\in{\cal B}_{0,i}
:

\displaystyle\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\beta}_{k}^{\alpha}),\;\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\beta}_{/i,k}^{\alpha})\geq 2\eta+\frac{1}{8}\alpha(1-\eta)\kappa_{0}.

3.

\forall i,\forall k\in{\cal B}_{1,i,+}
:

\left\lvert\int_{0}^{1}\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{/i,k}-t\Delta^{\alpha}_{/i,k})dt-2\eta\right\rvert\leq 2\alpha e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\kappa_{1}}.

4.

\forall i,\forall k\in{\cal B}_{1,i,+}
:

\left\lvert\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{k})-2\eta\right\rvert\leq 2\alpha e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\kappa_{1}},

\left\lvert\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\beta}_{/i,k}^{\alpha})-2\eta\right\rvert\leq 2\alpha e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\kappa_{1}}.

Note that the rate assumption on
\alpha
ensures that both rates hold in both Part 5 and Part 6 of Lemma
3
. The proof of Lemma
4
is presented in Section
5.3
. This lemma confirms that we have some control over the curvature of the regularizer on sets
{\cal B}_{0,i}
and
{\cal B}_{1,i,+}
. The following lemma enables us to obtain an upper bound for the error between the leave-one-out estimate and ALO by finding an upper bound on the error over the set
{\cal B}_{0}^{c}
. In other words, the next lemma enables us to remove the indices
k
for which we are certain
\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{k})
converge to infinity from our analysis.

Theorem 3.3
.

Suppose Assumptions A1-A5 hold, the conclusions of Lemma
3
are true, and
\alpha\geq\dfrac{4p\log(2)}{(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{\epsilon}})^{2}\eta(1-\eta)}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{/i}(t\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}+(1-t)\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})dt\bigg)^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i}

\displaystyle-\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}^{\top}\big(\lambda

\displaystyle{\rm diag}(\ddot{\underline{r}}_{{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}^{\alpha/i})+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{/i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\underline{\ell}}^{\alpha/i})\mathbf{X}_{/i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\dfrac{16\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}}{\lambda\alpha(1-\eta)\kappa_{0}}\Big(\frac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)\|\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|}{2\lambda\eta}+1\Big)^{2},

(34)

and similarly

\displaystyle~\bigg|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})\right)^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i}

\displaystyle~-\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{\utilde{r}}_{{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}^{\alpha/i})+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\utilde{\ell}}^{\alpha/i})\mathbf{X}_{{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}\bigg|

\displaystyle~\leq\dfrac{16\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}}{\lambda\alpha(1-\eta)\kappa_{0}}\Big(\frac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)\|\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|}{2\lambda\eta}+1\Big)^{2}

(35)

Here

\ddot{\underline{r}}_{k}^{\alpha/i}:=\int_{-1}^{0}(\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i}+t\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha}))_{k}dt
,

\,\,\ddot{\underline{\ell}}^{\alpha/i}_{k}:=\int_{-1}^{0}(\ddot{\ell}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i}+t\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha}))_{k}dt,

\,\,\ddot{\utilde{r}}^{\alpha/i}:=\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}-\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha})

and

\ddot{\utilde{\ell}}^{\alpha/i}:=\ddot{\ell}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}-\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha})
.

The complete proof of this theorem is presented in Section
5.4
.

Applying (
3.5
) and Theorem
3.3
, we have reduced the problem to the quadratic forms on the subset
{\cal B}_{0}^{c}
. The main remaining difficulty is that for the indices outside
{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}\setminus{\cal B}_{1,i,+}
we do not have much control over
\ddot{r}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\beta}^{\alpha}_{k})
. So the question is whether those terms can cause any issue in our approximations or not. Our next theorem will show that these elements will not cause any issue if there are not too many of them. More specifically, in the asymptotic regime we are interested in, i.e. the asymptotic regime in which
n,p
grow at the same rate, if the size of the set
|{\cal B}_{0,i}\setminus{\cal B}_{1,i,+}|
is sublinear in
n
, then the difference between ALO and LO converges to zero.

Theorem 3.4
.

Suppose the assumptions of Lemma
3
hold, and assume
\alpha=\omega\left(\frac{n{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\kappa_{0}^{2}\kappa_{1}^{2}\lambda(1-\eta))\eta}\right)
. Moreover, for a sufficiently large constant
C>0
, let
1\leq d_{n}\leq p/C
be such that

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}\setminus{\cal B}_{1,i,+}|\leq d_{n}

with probability at least
1-\tilde{q}_{n}
. Let
{\cal F}
denote a set such that
{\cal B}_{1,i,+}\subset{\cal F}\subset{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal F}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{\underline{r}}_{{\cal F}}^{\alpha/i})+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{{\cal F}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\underline{\ell}}^{\alpha/i})\mathbf{X}_{{\cal F}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal F}}

\displaystyle~-\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal F}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{\utilde{r}}_{{\cal F}}^{\alpha/i})+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{{\cal F}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\utilde{\ell}}^{\alpha/i})\mathbf{X}_{{\cal F}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal F}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{3}\eta^{3}(1\wedge\lambda\eta)^{3}}\sqrt{\dfrac{d_{n}}{n\lambda\eta}}+\dfrac{Cd_{n}}{n\lambda^{2}\eta^{2}}+\sqrt{\dfrac{C\log p}{n\lambda\eta}}

with probability at least
1-(n+1){\rm e}^{-\frac{p}{2}}-(n+2)p^{-d_{n}}-2q_{n}-2\check{q}_{n}-2\bar{q}_{n}-2\tilde{q}_{n}
, for sufficiently large
p
.

The proof of this claim is long and will be presented in Section
5.5
. We will now use this theorem to complete the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

First note that the leave-one-out cross validation risk of elastic net and smoothed elastic-net are

\displaystyle{\rm LO}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}).

\displaystyle{\rm LO}^{\alpha}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i}).

(36)

Hence the difference of the two is

\displaystyle|{\rm LO}(\lambda)-{\rm LO}^{\alpha}(\lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i,z_{i}}|\dot{\phi}(y_{i},z_{i})||\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i})|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i,z_{i}}|\dot{\phi}(y_{i},z_{i})|\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}_{/i})\|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i,z_{i}}|\dot{\phi}(y_{i},z_{i})|\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|\sqrt{\frac{4\log 2p}{\alpha\eta}}.

(37)

According to (
3.5
) we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}-{\rm LO}^{\alpha}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\lvert\dot{\phi}(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{i}^{\alpha})\right\rvert\cdot\dfrac{1}{n}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\Delta_{i}^{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}^{\alpha}\right\rvert,

(38)

Combining (
3.5
), (
3.5
), and (
3.5
), we have

\displaystyle~\left\lvert{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}-{\rm LO}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\max_{i,z_{i}}|\dot{\phi}(y_{i},z_{i})|\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|\sqrt{\frac{4\log 2p}{\alpha\eta}}+\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\lvert\dot{\phi}(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\beta}}_{i}^{\alpha})\right\rvert\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\Delta_{i}^{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}^{\alpha}\right\rvert.\ \ \ \

Furthermore, according to (
3.5
), Theorem
3.3
, and Theorem
3.4
we have that with probability at least
1-(n+1){\rm e}^{-\frac{p}{2}}-(n+2)p^{-d_{n}}-2q_{n}-2\check{q}_{n}-2\tilde{q}_{n}-2\bar{q}_{n}
:

\displaystyle~\left\lvert\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}^{\alpha}\right\rvert

(39)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,|\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\times

\displaystyle~\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bigg[\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{J}_{/i}(t\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}+(1-t)\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})dt\right)^{-1}-\left(\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})\right)^{-1}\bigg]\mathbf{x}_{i}

\displaystyle\,+|\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\left[\left(\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha})\right)^{-1}-\left(\mathbf{J}_{/i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{/i}^{\alpha}-\Delta_{/i}^{\alpha})\right)^{-1}\right]\mathbf{x}_{i},

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\times

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{\underline{r}}_{{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}^{\alpha/i})+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{/i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\underline{\ell}}^{\alpha/i})\mathbf{X}_{/i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}

\displaystyle-\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{\utilde{r}}_{{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}^{\alpha/i})+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\utilde{\ell}}^{\alpha/i})\mathbf{X}_{{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}}\Big|

\displaystyle+\dfrac{32|\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}}{\lambda\alpha(1-\eta)\kappa_{0}}\Big(\frac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)\|\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|}{2\lambda\eta}+1\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~|\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\times

\displaystyle\Bigg(\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{3}\eta^{3}(1\wedge\lambda\eta)^{3}}\sqrt{\dfrac{d_{n}\log^{2}p}{n\lambda\eta}}+\dfrac{Cd_{n}}{n\lambda^{2}\eta^{2}}+\sqrt{\dfrac{C\log p}{n\lambda\eta}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\dfrac{32|\dot{\ell}_{i}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}}{\lambda\alpha(1-\eta)\kappa_{0}}\Big(\frac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)\|\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|}{2\lambda\eta}+1\Big)^{2}.

(40)

As is clear from this equation, as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
, and for large values of
n,p
, if
d_{n}
grows slowly enough in
n
(or equivalently in
p
) the difference
\left\lvert{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}-{\rm LO}\right\rvert
will be negligible.
The last step of the proof, is to show that the difference between
\left\lvert{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}-{\rm ALO}\right\rvert
is also negligible. Note that our approximate ALO formula for elastic net can be written as

\displaystyle{\rm ALO}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{S}}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{S}}+\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{S}}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}),

(41)

where

\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}=\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})(2\lambda\eta\mathbb{I}+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{S}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{S}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{S}}.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle~|{\rm ALO}-{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\phi(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{S}}^{\top}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{S}}+\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}))-\phi(y_{i},(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}+\widehat{\Delta}^{\alpha}_{/i}))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\max_{i,z_{i}}|\dot{\phi}(y_{i},z_{i})||(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{S}}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{S}}|

\displaystyle+\max_{i,z_{i}}|\dot{\phi}(y_{i},z_{i})||\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{S}}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}^{\alpha}_{/i}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\max_{i,z_{i}}|\dot{\phi}(y_{i},z_{i})|\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|\sqrt{\frac{4\log 2p}{\alpha\eta}}

\displaystyle+\max_{i,z_{i}}|\dot{\phi}(y_{i},z_{i})||\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{S}}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}^{\alpha}_{/i}|,

(42)

where to obtain the last inequality we have used the first part of Lemma
3
.

Similar to the proof of Theorem
3.4
we can prove that

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{S}}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}-\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{1,i,+}}^{\top}(2\lambda\eta\mathbb{I}+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{1,i,+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{1,i,+}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{1,i,+}}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{3}\eta^{3}(1\wedge\lambda\eta)^{3}}\sqrt{\dfrac{d_{n}\log^{2}p}{n\lambda\eta}}+\dfrac{Cd_{n}}{n\lambda^{2}\eta^{2}}+\sqrt{\dfrac{C\log p}{n\lambda\eta}}

(43)

with probability at least
1-(n+1){\rm e}^{-\frac{p}{2}}-(n+2)p^{-d_{n}}-2q_{n}-2\check{q}_{n}-2\tilde{q}_{n}-2\bar{q}_{n}
, for sufficiently large
p
. Similarly,

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}^{\alpha}_{/i}-\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{1,i,+}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}^{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{1,i,+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{1,i,+}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{1,i,+}}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{3}\eta^{3}(1\wedge\lambda\eta)^{3}}\sqrt{\dfrac{d_{n}\log^{2}p}{n\lambda\eta}}+\dfrac{Cd_{n}}{n\lambda^{2}\eta^{2}}+\sqrt{\dfrac{C\log p}{n\lambda\eta}}

(44)

with probability at least
1-(n+1){\rm e}^{-\frac{p}{2}}-(n+2)p^{-d_{n}}-2q_{n}-2\check{q}_{n}-2\tilde{q}_{n}-2\bar{q}_{n}
, for sufficiently large
p
. In the rest of the proof, for the notational simplicity, we use the notation
\mathcal{B}_{+}
instead of
\mathcal{B}_{1,i,+}
.

\displaystyle|\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{S}}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}^{\alpha}_{/i}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~|\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}^{\top}(2\lambda\eta\mathbb{I}+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}

\displaystyle~-\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}^{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}|

\displaystyle+\dfrac{2{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{3}\eta^{3}(1\wedge\lambda\eta)^{3}}\sqrt{\dfrac{d_{n}\log^{2}p}{n\lambda\eta}}+\dfrac{Cd_{n}}{n\lambda^{2}\eta^{2}}+\sqrt{\dfrac{C\log p}{n\lambda\eta}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~|\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})|\times

\displaystyle\times\big|\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}^{\top}(2\lambda\eta\mathbb{I}+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}

\displaystyle~-\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}^{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}\big|

\displaystyle+|\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})-\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\times

\displaystyle\times\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}^{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{2{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{3}\eta^{3}(1\wedge\lambda\eta)^{3}}\sqrt{\dfrac{d_{n}\log^{2}p}{n\lambda\eta}}+\dfrac{Cd_{n}}{n\lambda^{2}\eta^{2}}+\sqrt{\dfrac{C\log p}{n\lambda\eta}}.

(45)

Since the minimum eigenvalue of
(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}^{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}})
is larger than
2\lambda\eta
, we can conclude that

\displaystyle|\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})-\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})|\times

\displaystyle\times\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}^{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}}{2\lambda\eta}|\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha})-\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}})|\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}}{2\lambda\eta}|\ddot{\ell}(\theta)(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|_{2}^{3}}{2\lambda\eta}|\ddot{\ell}(\bm{\theta})|\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|_{2}^{3}}{2\lambda\eta}|\ddot{\ell}(\bm{\theta})|\sqrt{\dfrac{4\log(2)p}{\alpha\eta}}

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda\eta}\sqrt{\dfrac{p}{\alpha\eta}},

(46)

with probability larger than
1-n{\rm e}^{-p}-q_{n}-\check{q}_{n}
.
To obtain Equality (a) we have used the mean value theorem and
\bm{\theta}=t\widehat{\bm{\beta}}+(1-t)\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}
for some
t\in[0,1]
. To obtain inequality (b) we have used Part 1 of Lemma
3
. Inequality (c) is based on Assumption A.4 along with Lemma
17
.

Also, using Lemma
13
we have that

\displaystyle|\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}^{\top}(2\lambda\eta\mathbb{I}+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}

\displaystyle-\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}^{\top}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}^{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{B}_{+}}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Gamma})}{(2\lambda\eta)^{2}}+\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}\lambda^{2}_{\max}(\bm{\Gamma})}{(2\eta\lambda)^{2}(2\lambda\eta-\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Gamma}))},

(47)

where

\displaystyle\bm{\Gamma}:=

\displaystyle~\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}^{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}

\displaystyle-2\lambda\eta\mathbb{I}+\mathbf{X}^{\top}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}))\mathbf{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}).

(48)

Therefore, by using Weyl’s theorem

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Gamma})\leq

\displaystyle~\lambda_{\max}(\lambda{\rm diag}(\ddot{r}_{\mathcal{B}_{+}}^{\alpha}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))-2\lambda\eta\mathbb{I})

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\max}({\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}))-{\rm diag}(\ddot{\ell}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}))\|\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|

\displaystyle{\leq}

\displaystyle~2\lambda\alpha{\rm e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\kappa_{1}}+\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\alpha}-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\|\|\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|{\rm PolyLog}(n)

\displaystyle{\leq}

\displaystyle~2\lambda\alpha{\rm e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\kappa_{1}}+\sqrt{\frac{4\log 2p}{\alpha\eta}}\|\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|{\rm PolyLog}(n)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{p}},

(49)

with probability larger than
1-{\rm e}^{-p}-\tilde{q}_{n}
.
To obtain the two penultimate two inqualities we have used Lemma
4
and (
10
). The last inequality uses
\alpha=\omega\left(\frac{p{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\eta}\wedge\frac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\kappa_{1}}\right)
, where we use the fact Lemma
19
to conclude that
\|\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|\leq(\sqrt{\gamma_{0}}+3)^{2}C_{X}
with probability at least
1-{\rm e}^{-p}
.

Plugging in these bounds into (
3.5
) we obtain

\displaystyle~|\mathbf{x}_{i,\mathcal{S}}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}_{/i}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\Delta}^{\alpha}_{/i}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~|\dot{\ell}(\widehat{\beta})|\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}}{p(2\lambda\eta)^{2}}+\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}}{(2p\eta\lambda)^{2}(\lambda\eta)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\sqrt{p}(\lambda\eta)(1\vee\lambda\eta)}\left(1+\dfrac{1}{p\lambda\eta}\right)+\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{3}\eta^{3}(1\wedge\lambda\eta)^{3}}\sqrt{\dfrac{d_{n}\log^{2}p}{n\lambda\eta}}+\dfrac{Cd_{n}}{n\lambda^{2}\eta^{2}}+\sqrt{\dfrac{C\log p}{n\lambda\eta}}

where we use
\alpha=\omega\left(\frac{p{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\eta}\wedge\frac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\kappa_{1}}\right)
.

Returning to (
3.5
) we thus write

\displaystyle~|{\rm ALO}-{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{3}\eta^{3}(1\wedge\lambda\eta)^{3}}\sqrt{\dfrac{d_{n}}{n\lambda\eta}}+\dfrac{d_{n}{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{n\lambda^{2}\eta^{2}}+\sqrt{\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{n\lambda\eta}},

by the assumption
\dot{\phi}(y_{i},z_{i})=O_{p}({\rm PolyLog}(n))
. To conclude the proof of the theorem, note that

\displaystyle~|{\rm ALO}-{\rm LO}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~|{\rm ALO}-{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}|+|{\rm ALO}^{\alpha}-{\rm LO}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\lambda^{3}\eta^{3}(1\wedge\lambda\eta)^{3}}\sqrt{\dfrac{d_{n}}{n\lambda\eta}}+\dfrac{d_{n}{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{n\lambda^{2}\eta^{2}}+\sqrt{\dfrac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{n\lambda\eta}}

provided
\alpha=\omega\left(\frac{n{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\kappa_{0}^{2}\kappa_{1}^{2}\lambda(1-\eta))\eta}\wedge\frac{{\rm PolyLog}(n)}{\kappa_{1}}\right)
, with probability at least
1-(n+1){\rm e}^{-p}-(n+2)p^{-d_{n}}-2q_{n}-2\check{q}_{n}-2\tilde{q}_{n}-2\bar{q}_{n}
. This finishes the proof of Theorem
3.2
.
∎

4
The example of linear regression

Theorem
3.2
shows that if
d_{n}
grows slowly enough (e.g.
d_{n}=p^{\alpha}
for
\alpha<1
), then the difference betweent ALO and LO will go to zero in proability. To see what the growth rate of
d_{n}
in terms of
p
is, in this section we focus on the concrete example of linear regression and obtain an upper bound for
|{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}\setminus{\cal B}_{1,i,+}|
. Even though the result of this section is given for the linear models, it is expected that a similar conclusion holds for more general models and under more general assumptions. However, given the length of the current paper, the complete investigation of the size of
|{\cal B}_{0,i}^{c}\setminus{\cal B}_{1,i,+}|
under the generalized linear model is left for a future research. Let us start with our modelling assumptions:

Assumption group B

B1

\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*}+\mathbf{w}
, where
\mathbf{w}\sim N(0,\sigma_{w}^{2}I)
is the noise or error in the observations.

B2

The loss function
l(y|\mathbf{x}^{\top}\bm{\beta})=\frac{1}{2}(y-\mathbf{x}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}

B3

The true coefficients
\bm{\beta}^{*}
satisfy

\frac{1}{p}\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\xi

for some constant
\xi>0
.

B4

\mathbf{X}
has iid entries
X_{ij}\sim N(0,\frac{1}{n})
.

B5

\lambda^{2}(1-\eta)^{2}=\omega(p^{-\frac{7}{12}})
.

Note that Assumptions B1-B3 are standard in the literature of linear regression. Assumption B4 is also frequently encountered in the high-dimensional asymptotic analysis of estimators
Miolane and Montanari 2021
;
Bradic and Chen 2015
;
Donoho et al. 2009a
;
Bayati and Montanari 2012
;
Weng et al. 2018
;
Dobriban and Wager 2018
;
Wang et al. 2020
;
Maleki et al. 2013
;
Thrampoulidis et al. 2015
;
Rangan 2011
;
Li and Wei 2021
. However, it is expected that this assumption can be relaxed as well given the more recent results in the literature
Celentano et al. 2020
. Finally, Assumption B5 is a technical assumption on the rate of
\lambda
. We note here that it shows that for large values of
p
, one can choose
\lambda
to be quite small. The following theorem uses Assumptions B1-B5 to find an upper bound for
({\cal B}_{1,i,+}\cup{\cal B}_{0,i})^{c}|
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Under Assumptions A1-A5 and B1-B5, in (
3.5
) set
\kappa_{0}=(\frac{8\log p}{cp})^{1/6}
and
\kappa_{1}=p^{-1/12}(\log p)^{1/4}
where
c
is the constant
c_{2}
in Lemma
8
. Then there exist constants
C,C^{\prime}>0
such that

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|({\cal B}_{1,i,+}\cup{\cal B}_{0,i})^{c}|\leq Cp^{11/12}(\log p)^{1/4}

with probability at least
1-C^{\prime}p^{-6}-q_{n}-{\rm e}^{-cp}
.

Sketch of the proof.

One of the main components of this proof uses concentration results on the empirical distribution of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
, the subgradient of
\ell_{1}
-regularizer (see below for precise definition), and the sparsity of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
, where
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
is the minimizer of

\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\eta\|\bm{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda(1-\eta)\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}.

In order to state the results, we first introduce the following terms:

•

Let
\widehat{\mu}
denotes the empirical distribution of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
.

•

Let
\Theta
be a random variable with its value uniformly distributed among the elements of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
, and let
Z\sim N(0,1)
and independent of
\Theta
.

•

Let
{\rm soft}(x,r)
denote the soft thresholding function

{\rm soft}(x,r)=(|x|-r)_{+}{\rm sign}(x).

•

For a couple
(\tau,b)
, define

\widehat{w}^{f}(\tau,b)=\frac{b}{b+2\lambda\eta\tau}{\rm soft}\Big(\tau Z+\Theta,\frac{\lambda(1-\eta)\tau}{b}\Big)-\Theta.

•

Let the couple
(\tau_{*},b_{*})
be the unique solution of the following equations:
‡
‡

‡

The uniqueness of the solution is proved in Lemma
23
.

\displaystyle\tau^{2}=

\displaystyle~\sigma^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}}\mathbb{E}[\widehat{w}^{f}(\beta,\tau)]^{2},

(50)

\displaystyle\beta=

\displaystyle~\tau-\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}}\mathbb{E}Z\cdot\widehat{w}^{f}(\beta,\tau).

(51)

•

Let
\mu^{*}
denote the law of the random variable
\widehat{w}^{f}(\tau_{*},b_{*})+\Theta
.

•

Let
s_{*}
be defined as

s_{*}=\mathbb{P}\left(|\Theta+\tau_{*}Z|\geq\frac{\lambda\tau_{*}}{b_{*}}\right).

Lemma 5

(restated and proved later as Theorem
A.2
)
.

Under Assumptions A1-A5 and B1-B5, there exist constants
C,c>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,0.5]
:

\mathbb{P}\left(W_{2}(\widehat{\mu},\mu^{*})^{2}>\varepsilon\right)\leq C\varepsilon^{-2}e^{-cp\varepsilon^{3}(\log\varepsilon)^{-2}},

where
W_{2}
denotes the Wasserstein 2-distance.

We can now start the proof of the theorem. By the definition of
{\cal B}_{0,i}
and
{\cal B}_{1,i}
