\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\min_{\mathbf{w}}\L(\mathbf{w})-\psi(\tau_{*},b_{*})\right|\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon}e^{-cn\varepsilon^{2}}.

∎

Lemma 29
.

There exists constants
C,c
depending only on
\Omega
such that for all closed set
D\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1]
:

\mathbb{P}(\min_{\mathbf{w}\in{\cal D}_{\epsilon}}{\cal C}(\mathbf{w})\leq\min_{\mathbf{w}}{\cal C}(\mathbf{w})+\varepsilon)\leq 2\mathbb{P}(\min_{\mathbf{w}\in{\cal D}_{\epsilon}}L(\mathbf{w})\leq\min_{\mathbf{w}}L(\mathbf{w})+3\varepsilon)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}e^{-cn\varepsilon^{2}}

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as the proof of Proposition C.1 in
Miolane and Montanari 2021
and thus omitted here.
∎

A.3
The asymptotic distribution and sparsity

As we mentioned in our paper we need to evaluate a few quantities such as the number of non-zero elements
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
. Clearly, the empirical disribution of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
can be used for this purpose. Hence, if we can evaluate what the empirical distribution of
\widehat{\beta}
converges to, then we can hopefully obtain accurate bounds on e.g.,
\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\|_{0}
.

Let
\widehat{\mu}
be the empirical distribution of the couple
(\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\bm{\beta}^{*})
. According to the results that have appeared in the approximate message framework and CGMT framework
Maleki 2010
;
Donoho et al. 2011b
;
Donoho et al. 2009b
;
Thrampoulidis et al. 2015
;
Thrampoulidis et al. 2018
;
Wang et al. 2022
, we expect
\widehat{\mu}
to converge to

\mu^{*}
that is the distribution of the couple

\Big(\frac{b_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}{\rm soft}\Big(\tau_{*}Z+\Theta,\frac{\lambda\tau_{*}}{b_{*}}\Big),\Theta\Big)

where
(Z,\Theta)\sim{\cal N}(0,1)\bigotimes\frac{1}{p}\sum\delta_{\beta_{k}^{*}}
, and
(\tau_{*},b_{*})
is the unique saddle point of
\psi(\tau,b)
defined in (
A.2
). The following theorem is a finite sample size confirmation of this claim:

Theorem A.2
.

There exists constants
C,c>0
depending only on
\Omega
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
,

\mathbb{P}\left(W_{2}(\widehat{\mu},\mu^{*})^{2}\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq C\varepsilon^{-2}e^{cp\varepsilon^{3}(\log\varepsilon)^{-2}}

Proof.

The proof of this Theorem is very similar to the proof of Theorem 3.1 of
Miolane and Montanari 2021
, and hence will be skipped. Please note that there will be some minor changes due to the fact that our regulaizer is elastic net, i.e.
\lambda\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}+\eta\|\bm{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}
compared to LASSO in
Miolane and Montanari 2021
, i.e.
\lambda\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}
. For that reason as we described in Section
A.4
, our scaler optimization function
\psi(\tau,b)
is slightly different from the corresponding function in
Miolane and Montanari 2021
.
∎

As we described before, one of our goals is to use
\widehat{\mu}
to evaluate the properties of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
. Hence, in most of our results we are more interested in the empirical law of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
, denotes as
\widehat{\mu}_{1}
, rather than
\widehat{\mu}
. However, it turns out that we can simply obtain a bound for
W_{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{1},\mu_{1}^{*})^{2}
, where
\mu^{*}_{1}
is the law of
\frac{b_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}soft(\tau_{*}Z+\Theta,\frac{\lambda\tau_{*}}{b_{*}})
using Theorem

A.2
.

Corollary A.3
.

There exists constants
C,c>0
depending only on
\Omega
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
,

\mathbb{P}\left(W_{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{1},\mu_{1}^{*})^{2}\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq C\varepsilon^{-2}e^{cp\varepsilon^{3}(\log\varepsilon)^{-2}}

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mu},\mu^{*})

\displaystyle=\underset{\scriptsize\begin{tabular}[]{c}$(X_{1},X_{2})\sim\widehat{\mu}$\\
$(Y_{1},Y_{2})\sim\mu^{*}$\end{tabular}}{\inf}\mathbb{E}[((X_{1}-Y_{1})^{2}+(X_{2}-Y_{2})^{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\underset{\scriptsize\begin{tabular}[]{c}$(X_{1},X_{2})\sim\widehat{\mu}$\\
$(Y_{1},Y_{2})\sim\mu^{*}$\end{tabular}}{\inf}\mathbb{E}(X_{1}-Y_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle=\underset{\scriptsize\begin{tabular}[]{c}$X_{1}\sim\widehat{\mu}_{1}$\\
$Y_{1}\sim\mu^{*}_{1}$\end{tabular}}{\inf}\mathbb{E}(X_{1}-Y_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle=W_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{1},\mu^{*}_{1})

(193)

Hence, (
A.3
) combined with Theorem
A.2
completes the proof.
∎

As we discussed in our main paper, we also need to bound the size of sets for which we have bounds on the magnitude of the subgradients of the
\ell_{1}
-norm. The rest of this section is dedicated to explaining how the sizes of such sets can be bounded. Define

\widehat{\mathbf{v}}:=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{y}-(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}+\eta\mathbf{I}_{p})\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\right]

where
\mathbf{I}_{p}
is the identity matrix on
\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
. It can be shown that
\widehat{\mathbf{v}}
is a subgradient of
\lVert\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\rVert_{1}
. In fact, the first order condition of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
gives

0\in\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\widehat{\bm{\beta}})+\lambda\partial\lVert\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\rVert_{1}+2\eta\widehat{\bm{\beta}}.

By simple algebra this is equivalent to

\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\in\partial\lVert\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\rVert_{1}.

Note that if
|\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}|<1
, then
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{i}
has to be zero. Hence, analyzing
\widehat{\mathbf{v}}
provides an upper bound on
\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\|_{0}
. Let
\mu^{*}_{1}
denote the distribution of

\frac{b_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}{\rm soft}(\tau_{*}Z+\Theta,\frac{\lambda\tau_{*}}{b_{*}})

as defined previously. Define

s_{*}=\mu_{*}(\{0\})=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\left[\Phi\left(\frac{\lambda}{b_{*}}-\frac{\beta_{k}^{*}}{\tau_{*}}\right)-\Phi\left(-\frac{\lambda}{b_{*}}-\frac{\beta_{k}^{*}}{\tau_{*}}\right)\right]

Note that if
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{i}\neq 0
then
|\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}|=1
. If there are not many zero coefficients with subgradients whose magnitude is close to
1
, we should expect,
\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}|=1\}}
to be close to
s^{*}
. The following theorem confirms this:

Theorem A.4
.

There exist constants
C,c>0
depending only on
\Omega
such that, for all
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}|\geq 1-\varepsilon\}}\geq s_{*}+2(1+\frac{\lambda}{b_{\min}})\varepsilon\right)\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}e^{-cn\varepsilon^{6}},

where
b_{\min}>0
is the lower bound of
b_{*}
in Lemma
24
.

Sketch of proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
25
using the convex Gaussian minimax theorem (CGMT) that was stated in (
180
), and is essentially the same as the proof of Theorem E.5 in
Miolane and Montanari 2021
. Therefore we provide a sketch of proof here while omitting the details. First we conctruct the primary optimization (PO) with
{\widehat{\mathbf{v}}}
being its unique optimizer. Then we identify the auxillary optimization (AO) of CGMT and study the local stability of AO, similar to Lemma
25
. Finally we use CGMT to connect the local stability of AO to that of PO.
Define the primary optimizatio (PO) as the following:

\displaystyle{\cal V}(\mathbf{v})=\min_{\mathbf{w}}\frac{1}{2n}\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{w}-\sigma\mathbf{z}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{n}\mathbf{v}^{\top}(\bm{\beta}^{*}+\mathbf{w})-\frac{\lambda}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}+\frac{\eta}{n}\|\mathbf{w}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{\eta}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

It can be verified using dual norm and interchangeability of min-max that

{\widehat{\mathbf{v}}}=\argmax_{\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}{\cal V}(\mathbf{v}).

Hence, the goal would be to use this optimization and CGMT to provide useful information about
{\widehat{\mathbf{v}}}
. As we described before, we expect
\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k}\rm{1}_{\{|v_{k}|\geq 1-\varepsilon\}}
to be close to the number of nonzero coefficients and that should be close to
s_{*}
. Hence, we set

D_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{\mathbf{v}:\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k}\rm{1}_{\{|v_{k}|\geq 1-\varepsilon\}}\geq s_{*}+2\left(1+\frac{\lambda}{b_{min}}\right)\varepsilon\right\}.

We have

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}|\geq 1-\varepsilon\}}\geq s_{*}+2(1+\frac{\lambda}{b_{\min}})\varepsilon\right)=\mathbb{P}({\widehat{\mathbf{v}}}\in D_{\varepsilon})\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{D_{\varepsilon}}{\cal V}(\mathbf{v})\geq\max_{\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}{\cal V}(\mathbf{v})-\varepsilon^{\prime}\right).

for any
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
. Note that
\varepsilon^{\prime}
will be decided after the analysis of PO is finished. Using the same arguments as the ones presented in Section
A.2
, we can obtain the following auxillary optimization for this problem:

\displaystyle V(\mathbf{v})=

\displaystyle~\min_{\mathbf{w}}\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\lVert\mathbf{w}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{n}+\sigma^{2}}\frac{\lVert\mathbf{h}\rVert_{2}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{w}+\frac{g^{\prime}\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\right)_{+}^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{n}\left(\mathbf{v}^{\top}(\mathbf{w}+\bm{\beta}^{*})-\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\eta}{n}\left(\lVert\mathbf{w}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

(195)

where
\mathbf{g}\sim N(0,\mathbb{I}_{p})
,
\mathbf{h}\sim N(0,\mathbb{I}_{n})
and
g^{\prime}\sim N(0,1)
, independent with each other. Directly working with
D_{\varepsilon}
is quite difficult, but recall in Lemma
25
we have defined a set
\{\mathbf{w}:\frac{1}{p}\|\mathbf{w}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}>\varepsilon\}
and it was easier to work with. In fact, we can define a similar set

\tilde{D}_{\varepsilon}:=\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\frac{1}{p}\|\mathbf{v}-{\widehat{\mathbf{v}}}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\varepsilon\},

with

{\widehat{\mathbf{v}}}^{f}:=-\frac{b_{*}}{\lambda\tau_{*}}({\widehat{\mathbf{w}}}^{f}-\tau_{*}\mathbf{g})-\frac{2\eta}{\lambda}({\widehat{\mathbf{w}}}^{f}+\bm{\beta}^{*}).

And one can then show that, for some constants
C,c,\gamma>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\max_{\mathbf{v}\in\tilde{D_{\varepsilon}}}V(\mathbf{v})\geq\max_{\mathbf{v}}V(\mathbf{v})-c\varepsilon)\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon}e^{-cn\varepsilon^{2}}

(196)

The proof is essentially the same as that of Theorem E.7 of
Miolane and Montanari 2021
and thus omitted here.

The next step is to substitute the
\tilde{D}_{\varepsilon}
in (
196
) back to
D_{\varepsilon}
. The goal is to prove that, for some constants
C,c,\gamma>0
, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
:

\mathbb{P}(\max_{\mathbf{v}\in D_{\varepsilon}}V(\mathbf{v})\geq\max_{\mathbf{v}}V(\mathbf{v})-3\gamma\varepsilon^{3})\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}e^{-cn\varepsilon^{6}}

The proof is essentially the same as that of Lemma E.9 of
Miolane and Montanari 2021
and thus omitted.

Then we connect (
A.3
) with its
{\cal V}(\mathbf{v})
version via CGMT. Notice that by interchanging min-max and using dual norm expressions, we have

\max_{\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}{\cal V}(\mathbf{v})=\min_{\mathbf{w}}{\cal C}(\mathbf{w}),\quad\max_{\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}V(\mathbf{v})=\min_{\mathbf{w}}L(\mathbf{w}).

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{D_{\varepsilon}}{\cal V}(\mathbf{v})\geq\max_{\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}{\cal V}(\mathbf{v})-3\gamma\varepsilon^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}{\cal V}(\mathbf{v})<\psi(\tau_{*},b_{*})-\gamma\varepsilon^{3}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{D_{\varepsilon}}{\cal V}(\mathbf{v})\geq\psi(\tau_{*},b_{*})-2\gamma\varepsilon^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathbb{P}\left(\min_{\mathbf{w}}{\cal C}(\mathbf{w})<\psi(\tau_{*},b_{*})-\gamma\varepsilon^{3}\right)+2\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{D_{\varepsilon}}V(\mathbf{v})\geq\psi(\tau_{*},b_{*})-2\gamma\varepsilon^{3}\right).

(198)

Now by Lemma
28
, the first term is bounded by
\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}e^{-cn\varepsilon^{6}}
. For the second term, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{D_{\varepsilon}}V(\mathbf{v})\geq\psi(\tau_{*},b_{*})-2\gamma\varepsilon^{3}\right)

(199)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{D_{\varepsilon}}V(\mathbf{v})\geq\max_{\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}V(\mathbf{v})-3\gamma\varepsilon^{3}\right)+\mathbb{P}(\max_{\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}V(\mathbf{v})>\psi(\tau_{*},b_{*})+\gamma\varepsilon^{3})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}e^{-cn\varepsilon^{6}}+\mathbb{P}(\min_{\mathbf{w}}L(\mathbf{w})>\psi(\tau_{*},b_{*})+\gamma\varepsilon^{3})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}e^{-cn\varepsilon^{6}}+\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}e^{-cn\varepsilon^{6}}

The penultimate inequality uses (
A.3
) and the last inequality uses Corollary
A.1
. Putting (
A.3
), (
198
) and (
199
) together we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}|\geq 1-\varepsilon\}}\geq s_{*}+2(1+\frac{\lambda}{b_{\min}})\varepsilon\right)\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}e^{-cn\varepsilon^{6}}.

∎

The final theorem that we would like to mention in the appendix is a concentration result on the number of nonzero elements of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
.

Theorem A.5
.

There exist constants
C,c
depending only on
\Omega
such that for all
0<\varepsilon<1
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lvert\frac{1}{p}\lVert\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\rVert_{0}-s_{*}\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq C\varepsilon^{-6}e^{-cp\varepsilon^{6}}

Proof.

Note that if an elements of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{i}\neq 0
, then its corresponding subgradient has to be either
1
or
-1
. Hence, the upper bound of
\frac{1}{p}\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\|_{0}
is a direct result of Lemma
A.4
. For the lower bound, the proof follows a similar PO-AO-local stability path and is essentially the same as the proof of Theorem F.1 in
Miolane and Montanari 2021
.
∎

A.4
Study of the Scalar Optimization

Let
h_{r}(x)
be the Huber loss

h_{r}(x):=\frac{1}{2r}\lVert{\rm soft}(\mathbf{x},r)-\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert{\rm soft}(\mathbf{x},r)\rVert_{1}.

We remind the reader that

\displaystyle\psi(\tau,b)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\tau}+\tau\right)\frac{b}{2}-\frac{b^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\min_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\{\frac{b}{2\tau}\lVert\mathbf{w}\rVert_{2}^{2}-b\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{w}+\lambda\left(\lVert\mathbf{w}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}\right)+\eta\left(\lVert\mathbf{w}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\right\},

(200)

and that

\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}(\tau,b):=\frac{b}{b+2\eta\tau}{\rm soft}\left(\tau\mathbf{g}+\bm{\beta}^{*},\frac{\lambda\tau}{b}\right)-\bm{\beta}^{*}

Inserting back
\mathbf{w}=\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}(\tau,b)
, it can be shown that

\psi(\tau,b)=\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\tau^{\prime}}+\frac{b\tau^{\prime}}{b-2\eta\tau^{\prime}}\right)\frac{b}{2}-\frac{b^{2}}{2}-\eta\sigma^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{n}\mathbb{E}h_{\frac{\lambda\tau^{\prime}}{b}}(\tau^{\prime}\tilde{\mathbf{g}}+\bm{\beta}^{*})-\frac{\lambda}{n}\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}-\frac{b\tau^{\prime}}{2n}\mathbb{E}\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{g}}\rVert_{2}^{2}

where
\tau^{\prime}=\frac{b}{b+2\eta\tau}\tau
and
\tilde{\mathbf{g}}=\mathbf{g}-\frac{2\eta}{b}\bm{\beta}^{*}
.
The variable
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}
and function
\psi(\tau,b)
play an important role in our analysis later. In this section, we study the saddle point of
\psi(\tau,b)
:

(\tau_{*},b_{*}):=\underset{b\geq 0}{\argmax}\;\underset{\tau\geq\sigma}{\argmin}\psi(\tau,b)

In the rest of this section we prove Lemma
23
and Lemma
24
.

Proof of Lemma
23
.

Note that
\psi
is convex-concave and differentiable with respect to
(\tau,b)
, and the differentiation can be taken inside the expectation. Using the formulae

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial r}h_{r}(x)=-\frac{1}{2r^{2}}[{\rm soft}(x,r)-x]^{2}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}h_{r}(x)=\frac{1}{r}\left(x-{\rm soft}(x,r)\right)

one can obtain the following:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\psi(\tau,b)=\frac{b}{2\tau^{2}}\left(\tau^{2}-\sigma^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\lVert\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}(\tau,b)\rVert_{2}^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial b}\psi(\tau,b)=\tau-b-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}(\tau,b).

First, for each
b\geq 0
consider
\min_{\tau\geq\sigma}\psi(\tau,b)
. Let
f_{b}(\tau)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\psi(\tau,b)
. We have

f_{b}(\tau)=\frac{b}{2}\left(1-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\tau^{2}}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{b}{b+2\eta\tau}{\rm soft}(g_{k}+\frac{\beta_{k}^{*}}{\tau},\frac{\lambda}{b})-\frac{\beta_{k}^{*}}{\tau}\right]^{2}\right).

Hence, we have

•

f_{b}(\tau)
is differentiable since the integrand is almost surely differentiable, and the distribution of
\mathbf{g}
is continuous

•

f^{\prime}_{b}(\tau)>0
because
\psi(\cdot,b)
is strictly convex

•

f_{b}(\sigma)=-\frac{b}{2n}\mathbb{E}\|\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}(\tau,b)\|^{2}<0
since
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}(\tau,b)
is non-degenerate

•

f_{b}(+\infty)=1
using the Dominated Convergence Theorem.

Therefore
\forall b\geq 0
,
\exists
unique
\tau_{0}(b)>\sigma
such that
f_{b}(\tau_{0}(b))=0
. Moreover
f_{b}(\tau)<0
on the left and
>0
on the right. Hence,
\tau_{0}(b)
is the unique minimizer of
\psi(\tau,b)
at
b
. Moreover, by the Implicit Function Theorem,
\tau_{0}(b)
is differentiable.

Next we define
G(b)=\psi(\tau_{0}(b),b)=\min_{\tau\geq\sigma}\psi(\tau,b)
so that

\max_{b\geq 0}\min_{\tau\geq\sigma}\psi(\tau,b)=\max_{b\geq 0}G(b)

Its derivative is given by

\displaystyle g(b):

\displaystyle=G^{\prime}(b)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial b}\psi(\tau,b)\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}(b)}+\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\psi(\tau,b)\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}(b)}\tau_{0}^{\prime}(b)

\displaystyle=\tau_{0}(b)-b-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}(\tau_{0}(b),b)

The last line is because
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\psi(\tau,b)\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}(b)}=0
. Next we show that
G(b)
has a unique maximizer
b_{*}>0
and it is also the unique solution of
g(b)=0
.

•

g(b)
is a decreasing function, because
G(b)
is the pointwise minimum of a collection of strictly-concave functions, and is hence strictly-concave itself

•

\lim\inf_{b\to 0_{+}}g(b)\geq\sigma
. To see this, first notice that
\tau_{0}(b)
is the zero of
\frac{2}{b}f_{b}(\tau)
and

\lim_{b\to 0_{+}}\frac{2}{b}f_{b}(\tau)=1-\tau^{-2}(\sigma^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2}).

Therefore, we have

\lim_{b\to 0_{+}}\tau_{0}(b)=\sigma^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2},

By using Fatou’s lemma we obtain

\displaystyle\lim\inf_{b\to 0_{+}}g(b)

\displaystyle=\lim_{b\to 0_{+}}\tau_{0}(b)-\lim\sup_{b\to 0_{+}}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}(\tau_{0}(b),b)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{k}\left(0-\beta_{k}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sigma^{2}.

•

As mentioned before
G(b)
is the pointwise minimum of a collection of strictly concave functions so it is itself strictly concave and therefore admits a unique maximizer
b_{*}\geq 0
. By the last point
\lim\inf_{b\to 0_{+}}g(b)\geq\sigma^{2}>0
. Therefore the maximizer cannot be
0
, and hence
b_{*}>0
. Since
G(b)
is differentiable
b_{*}
must be a zero of
g(b)
, and the zero must be unique as the maximizer is unique.

We conclude that the unique saddle point is
(\tau_{0}(b_{*}),b_{*})
and it satisfies (
23
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
24
.

We remind the reader of the following notation:

\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}:=\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}(\tau_{*},b_{*}).

We divide the proof into the following steps:

Step 1 (Lower bound for
b_{*}
):

Using the same notations as the ones used in the proof of Lemma
23
, we have
G(b)=\min_{\tau\geq\sigma}\psi(\tau,b)
and
g(b)=G^{\prime}(b)
. Using the fact that
\mathbb{E}Z\cdot{\rm soft}(Z+a,r)=\Phi(a-r)+\Phi(-a-r)
(which can be verified directly via integration), we have

\displaystyle g(b)=G^{\prime}(b)

\displaystyle=\tau_{0}(b)-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}(\tau_{0}(b),b)-b

\displaystyle=\tau_{0}(b)\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\frac{b}{b+2\eta\tau_{0}(b)}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}g_{k}\cdot{\rm soft}(g_{k}+\frac{\theta_{k}^{*}}{\tau_{0}(b)},\frac{\lambda}{b})\right)-b

\displaystyle=\tau_{0}(b)\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\frac{b}{b+2\eta\tau_{0}(b)}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\left[\Phi(\frac{\theta_{k}^{*}}{\tau_{0}(b)}-\frac{\lambda}{b})+\Phi(-\frac{\theta_{k}^{*}}{\tau_{0}(b)}-\frac{\lambda}{b})\right]\right)-b

\displaystyle\geq\tau_{0}(b)\left(1-\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(\frac{\Theta}{\tau_{0}(b)}-\frac{\lambda}{b})+\Phi(-\frac{\Theta}{\tau_{0}(b)}-\frac{\lambda}{b})\right]\right)-b

\displaystyle:=\tau_{0}(b)\left(1-\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}}\mathbb{E}h_{b}(\frac{\Theta}{\tau_{0}(b)})\right)-b,

where
\Theta
has uniform distribution over the elements of
\beta^{*}
, and
h_{b}(x)=\Phi(x-\alpha)+\Phi(-x-\alpha)
is an even funcion, decreasing for
x<0
and increasing for
x\geq 0
. Let
K=\frac{2\xi}{\sigma\sqrt{\gamma_{0}}}
. By Markov inequality we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}h_{b}\left(\frac{\Theta}{\tau_{0}(b)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq h_{b}(K)+\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{\Theta}{\tau_{0}(b)}\right|\geq K\right)

\displaystyle\leq h_{b}(K)+\frac{\mathbb{E}\Theta^{2}}{\tau_{0}^{2}(b)K^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq h_{b}(K)+\frac{\gamma_{0}}{4}.

Finally it can be verified directly that
\lim_{b\to 0_{+}}h_{b}(K)=0
. Henec we can find
b_{0}>0
depending only on
\xi,\lambda,\sigma,\gamma_{0}
such that
\forall b\leq b_{0},h_{b}(K)\leq\frac{\gamma_{0}}{4}
. Hence
\forall b\leq b_{0}
we have

\displaystyle g(b)

\displaystyle\geq\tau_{0}(b)(1-\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}}(\frac{\gamma_{0}}{4}+\frac{\gamma_{0}}{4}))-b

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\tau_{0}(b)-b

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sigma}{2}-b.

If we let
b_{min}=\min\{b_{0},\frac{\sigma}{4}\}
, then
\forall b\leq b_{min}
,
g(b)\geq\frac{\sigma}{4}>0
. Since
b_{*}
is the zero of
g(b)
and
g(b)
is decreasing, we conclude that
b_{*}>b_{min}
. This finishes the proof of the first step.

Step 2 (
\tau_{*}<\tau_{max}
):

First note that if we insert
\mathbf{w}=0
in the definition of
\psi(\tau,b)
, then we will have
\psi(\tau,b)\leq\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\tau}+\tau\right)\frac{b}{2}-\frac{b^{2}}{2}
. Hence,

\psi(\tau_{*},b_{*})\leq\max_{b\geq 0}\min_{\tau\geq\sigma}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\tau}+\tau\right)\frac{b}{2}-\frac{b^{2}}{2}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}.

Next, we show that
\psi(\tau,b)
has an increasing lower bound
g(\tau)
for all
b\geq b_{min}
. In fact,

\displaystyle\psi(\tau,b)=\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\tau}+\tau\right)\frac{b}{2}-\frac{b^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\{\lVert\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}\rVert_{2}^{2}(\frac{b}{2\tau}+\eta)-(b\mathbf{g}-2\eta\beta^{*})^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}+\lambda\lVert\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}-\lambda\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}\right\}

(202)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{b}{2\tau}(\sigma^{2}+\tau^{2})-\frac{b^{2}}{2}+(\frac{b}{2\tau}+\eta)(\tau^{2}-\sigma^{2})-b(\tau-b)+\frac{2\eta}{n}\mathbb{E}\bm{\beta}^{*\top}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}+\frac{\lambda}{n}\mathbb{E}\lVert\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}-\frac{\lambda}{n}\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta(\tau^{2}-\sigma^{2})+\frac{b^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\eta}{n}\mathbb{E}\bm{\beta}^{*\top}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}+\frac{\lambda}{n}\mathbb{E}\lVert\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}-\frac{\lambda}{n}\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}.

The first two terms in the last line of the above equation are nonnegative when
\tau=\tau_{*}
. For the third term,

\displaystyle\frac{2\eta}{n}\mathbb{E}\bm{\beta}^{*\top}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\eta}{n}\frac{b_{*}\tau_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\sum_{i}\beta_{i}^{*}\cdot\mathbb{E}{\rm soft}\left(Z+\frac{\beta_{i}^{*}}{\tau_{*}},\frac{\lambda}{b_{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{2\eta}{n}\frac{b_{*}\tau_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\sum|\beta_{i}^{*}|\cdot\left(\frac{|\beta_{i}^{*}|}{\tau_{*}}+\frac{\lambda}{b_{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{2\eta}{n}\frac{b_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}--\frac{2\eta}{n}\frac{\lambda\tau_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\lVert\bm{\beta}^{*}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{2\eta_{\max}}{\delta}\xi^{2}-\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\delta}\xi

\displaystyle:=-C_{1}

(203)

In the above equations, the first inequality uses
|\mathbb{E}{\rm soft}(Z+x,r)|\leq|x|+r
, the third uses Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and the last uses
\frac{b_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\leq 1
,
\frac{\eta\tau_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

For the fourth term of (
202
), using the notation
\Theta
taking values uniformly from the elements of
\bm{\beta}^{*}
and
Z\sim N(0,1)
and independent from
\Theta
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{n}\mathbb{E}\|\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{\gamma_{0}}\frac{b_{*}\tau_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\mathbb{E}\left|{\rm soft}(Z+\frac{\Theta}{\tau_{*}},\frac{\lambda}{b_{*}})\right|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{\gamma_{0}}\frac{b_{\min}\tau_{*}}{b_{\min}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\mathbb{E}\left|{\rm soft}(Z+\frac{\Theta}{\tau_{*}},\frac{\lambda}{b_{\min}})\right|.

Now consider the function
h(a)=\mathbb{E}|{\rm soft}(Z+a,r)|
with
r=\frac{\lambda}{b_{min}}
. We have
h(0)=\mathbb{E}|{\rm soft}(Z,r)|:=C>0
and by Monotone Convergence Theorem we have
\lim\limits_{a\to\infty}h(a)=+\infty
. Therefore there exists a constant
C_{2}>0
that depends only on
r=\frac{\lambda}{b_{\min}}
such that
h(a)\geq C_{2}
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{n}\mathbb{E}\|\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}+\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{\gamma_{0}}\frac{b_{min}\tau_{*}}{b_{min}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\mathbb{E}h(\frac{\Theta}{\tau_{*}})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{\gamma_{0}}\frac{b_{min}\tau_{*}}{b_{min}+2\eta\tau_{*}}C_{2}

(204)

Finally, for the last term of (
202
) we have

\frac{\lambda}{n}\|\bm{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}\leq\frac{\lambda\xi}{\gamma_{0}}\leq\frac{\lambda_{\max}\xi}{\gamma_{0}}.

Combining (
A.4
), (
203
), (
204
) and (
A.4
) together, we have

\eta(\tau_{*}^{2}-\sigma^{2})-C_{1}+\frac{\lambda}{\gamma_{0}}\frac{b_{\min}\tau_{*}}{b_{\min}+2\eta\tau_{*}}C_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{\max}\xi}{\gamma_{0}}\leq\psi(\tau_{*},b_{*})\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}

If we define
C_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{\ sigma^{2}}{2}+\eta_{\max}\sigma^{2}+C_{1}+\frac{\lambda_{\max}\xi}{\gamma_{0}}
,
C_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\gamma_{0}}b_{\min}C_{2}
, we can rephrase the above inequality into

\eta\tau_{*}^{2}+\frac{C_{2}^{\prime}\tau_{*}}{b_{\min}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\leq C_{1}^{\prime}

It can be verified directly that, the minimum of the left hand side over
\eta\geq 0
is
\max\{(\sqrt{2C_{2}^{\prime}}-\frac{b_{\min}}{2}),\frac{C_{2}^{\prime}}{b_{\min}}\}\tau_{*}:=C_{3}\tau_{*}
. Hence, we conclude that
\tau_{*}<\tau_{\max}=\frac{C_{1}^{\prime}}{C_{3}}
depending on model parameters.

Step 3 (Upper bound for
b_{*}
):

First note that

\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}=\frac{1}{n}\frac{b_{*}\tau_{*}}{b_{*}+2\eta\tau_{*}}\sum_{i}\mathbb{P}\left(\left|g_{i}+\frac{\beta_{i}^{*}}{\tau_{*}}\right|>\frac{\lambda}{b_{*}}\right)\geq 0.

Therefore

b_{*}=\tau_{*}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{f}\leq\tau_{max}.

Step 4 (Lower bound for
\tau_{*}
):

It is trivial that
\tau_{*}>\sigma
and is hence skipped.
∎
